[2504.20877] Preference-centric Bandits:Optimality of Mixtures and Regret-efficient Algorithms Preference-centric Bandits: Optimality of Mixtures and Regret-efficient Algorithms Meltem Tatlı Thanks: Meltem Tatlı and Ali Tajer are with the Department of Electrical, Computer, and Systems Engineering, Rensselaer Polytechnic Institute. Arpan Mukherjee Thanks: Arpan Mukherjee completed this work while he was with the Department of Electrical, Computer, and Systems Engineering, Rensselaer Polytechnic Institute. He is currently with the Department of Electrical and Electronic Engineering, Imperial College London. Prashanth L.A Thanks: Prashanth L.A. is with the Department of Computer Science and Engineering, Indian Institute of Technology Madras. Karthikeyan Shanmugam Thanks: Karthikeyan Shanmugam is with Google Research India. Ali Tajer 1 1 footnotemark: 1 Abstract The objective of canonical multi-armed bandits is to identify and repeatedly select an arm with the largest reward, often in the form of the expected value of the arm’s probability distribution. Such a utilitarian perspective and focus on the probability models’ first moments, however, is agnostic to the distributions’ tail behavior and their implications for variability and risks in decision-making. For instance, in high-stakes applications like clinical trials or autonomous control, an arm with a slightly lower mean but significantly lower probability of catastrophic outcomes may be far preferable. This paper introduces a principled framework for shifting from expectation-based evaluation to an alternative reward formulation, termed a preference metric (PM). The PMs can place the desired emphasis on different reward realization and can encode a richer modeling of preferences that incorporate risk aversion, robustness, or other desired attitudes toward uncertainty. A fundamentally distinct observation in such a PM-centric perspective is that designing bandit algorithms will have a significantly different principle: as opposed to the reward-based models in which the optimal sampling policy converges to repeatedly sampling from the single best arm, in the PM-centric framework the optimal policy converges to selecting a mix of arms based on a specific and well-defined mixing weights. Designing such mixture policies departs from the principles for designing bandit algorithms in significant ways, primarily because of uncountable mixture possibilities. The paper formalizes the PM-centric bandit framework and offers two broad classes of algorithms for the horizon-dependent and infinite-horizon (anytime) settings. These algorithms are capable of learning and tracking mixtures in a regret-efficient fashion. These algorithms have two distinctions from their canonical counterparts: (i) they involve an estimation routine to form reliable estimates of optimal mixtures, and (ii) they are equipped with tracking mechanisms to navigate arm selection fractions to track the optimal mixtures. These algorithms’ regret guarantees are investigated under various algebraic forms of the PMs. Finally, empirical experiments are conducted to demonstrate the regret performance of the proposed algorithms. † † footnotetext: The results have been partially presented at the International Conference on Artificial Intelligence and Statistics (AISTATS) 2025. 1 Introduction The canonical objective of multi-armed bandits (MABs) is to design a sequence of experiments to identify or repeatedly select the arm with the highest expected reward. While this utilitarian view provides a simple and meaningful metric to compare and rank the arms, relying only on the expected value abstracts away critical information about the underlying reward distributions. Specifically, summarizing each arm by its expected value — computed as the integral of the complement of its cumulative distribution function (CDF) — ignores aspects such as risk, variability, and tail behavior, which can be crucial in real-world decision-making. For instance, in high-stakes applications like clinical trials, finance, or autonomous control, an arm with a slightly lower mean but significantly lower probability of catastrophic outcomes may be far preferable. Motivated by this observation, we propose a principled shift from expectation-based evaluation to distorted expectation, where we apply a transformation (or distortion) to the complement CDF before integrating. Such distortion places the desired emphasis on different reward realization, and has the versatility to encode a richer modeling of preferences that incorporate risk aversion, robustness, or other desired attitudes toward uncertainty. By embedding distortion functions directly into the reward criterion, we open new avenues for adaptive learning algorithms that align more closely with complex real-world objectives. This approach naturally balances the tradeoff between utility and other metrics of interest — such as risk sensitivity, robustness to outliers, and tail behavior — by shaping the decision criterion through the distortion function. Unlike averaging, which treats all realizations uniformly, distorting the complement CDF allows us to amplify or attenuate the influence of specific parts of the distribution (e.g., penalizing low outcomes or overweighting high-confidence gains). By carefully selecting the distortion function, one can interpolate between purely utilitarian strategies and highly conservative or risk-seeking policies, making this framework highly adaptable to diverse application needs. A natural and general way to formalize these distortions is through the Choquet integral, which provides a framework for aggregating outcomes with respect to non-additive measures. Unlike the standard expectation, which is a linear operator over probabilities, the Choquet integral allows for weighting different regions of the outcome space non-linearly, making it particularly well-suited for modeling preferences, encoding pessimism, optimism, or ambiguity aversion directly into the evaluation of an arm’s performance. This approach enables nuanced tradeoffs between utility and robustness, allowing the decision-maker to account for rare but impactful events or to safeguard against worst-case scenarios, all within a unified and interpretable mathematical framework. Preference Metric. To formalize these, we adopt a distortion function function h : [ 0 , 1 ] → [ 0 , 1 ] h:[0,1]\to[0,1] that satisfies h ⁡ ( 0 ) = 0 h(0)=0 to apply desired distortions to any probability distribution. Based on this, corresponding to any given CDF ℚ \mathbb{Q} , we define preference metrics (PMs) of ℚ \mathbb{Q} , denoted by U h U_{h} as the signed Choquet integral , which was first introduced in the capacity-theory setting [ 1 ] , later given a decision-theoretic integral representation [ 2 ] , and more recently studied in risk-management concept [ 3 ] . The PM U h U_{h} associated with ℚ \mathbb{Q} is specified by U h ( ℚ ) ≜ ∫ − ∞ 0 \displaystyle U_{h}(\mathbb{Q})\triangleq\int_{-\infty}^{0} ( h ⁡ ( 1 − ℚ ⁡ ( x ) ) − h ⁡ ( 1 ) ) ​ 𝑑 x + ∫ 0 ∞ h ⁡ ( 1 − ℚ ⁡ ( x ) ) ​ 𝑑 x . \displaystyle\Big(h\big(1-\mathbb{Q}(x)\big)-h(1)\Big)\,{\rm d}x+\int_{0}^{\infty}h\big(1-\mathbb{Q}(x)\big)\,{\rm d}x\ . (1) The distortion function h h enables high versatility in specifying and unifying various notions of preference. Given this notion of PM, it can replace the conventional expected value as the desired metric. Subsequently, the objective of a bandit algorithm becomes to identify the arm with the largest PM. We note that setting h ⁡ ( u ) = u h(u)=u , the PM associated with distribution ℚ \mathbb{Q} reduces to its expected value. Hence, the PM-centric bandit framework subsumes the conventional mean-based bandit framework. The choice of PM depends on the nature of the problem, and it can be designed to emphasize certain distributional characteristics, e.g., tail behavior, over others, e.g., the mean. To illustrate the effect of distortion, we provide two examples. Example 1 (Inverted- S S ) A procurement officer repeatedly selects among suppliers whose delivery times have heavy - tailed delays due to strikes, weather, or geopolitical shocks. Adopting the inverted- S S distortion function h ⁡ ( u ) = exp ⁡ [ − ( − ln ⁡ u ) β ] , where u ∈ ( 0 , 1 ] , β &gt; 0 , \displaystyle h(u)=\exp\Big[-\big(-\ln u\big)^{\beta}\Big]\ ,\qquad\mbox{where}\quad u\in(0,1]\ ,\;\beta&gt;0\ , (2) balances a tradeoff between utility and delay and yields a smooth, prospect - theoretic metric that accentuates precisely those parts of the loss–gain distribution the decision - maker values. Specifically, • With β &lt; 1 \beta&lt;1 , the distortion function over - weights the small probabilities of extreme delays, steering the algorithm toward suppliers with shallower tail risk, even at slightly higher mean cost • If later contracts guarantee compensation for catastrophic delays, setting β &gt; 1 \beta&gt;1 under - weights those rare events, allowing the agent to prioritize lower expected cost instead. Figure 1 showcases applying this distortion function on light-tailed (Gaussian and exponential) and heavy-tailed (lognormal) distributions. Figure 1 : Distorted complement CDFs by Inverted- S S distortion. There exists extensive literature on PMs. These include L L -functionals in robust location estimation [ 4 ] , Yaari’s dual utilities in decision theory [ 5 ] , distorted premium principles in insurance [ 6 ] , and distortion risk measures in finance [ 7 ] . Some widely used examples of PMs are risk measures such value-at-risk (VaR), expected shortfall (ES) [ 8 ] , and conditional VaR (CVaR) [ 9 ] , Gini shortfall [ 10 ] ; measures of variability, such as deviation measures [ 11 ] , mean-median deviation, inter-quantile range, Wang’s right-tail deviation, inter-expected shortfall, Gini deviation [ 12 ] , cumulative Tsallis past entropy [ 13 ] . PM-centric Optimal Policy. The utilitarian MAB settings often adopt the tacit assumption that there exists a single optimal arm that maximizes the desired reward function. This premise, subsequently, guides the design of regret-efficient algorithms focused on identifying and repeatedly selecting the single best arm. In a sharp contrast, in the PM-centric framework, we show that the assumption that a single arm achieves the optimal PM is not necessarily valid. Specifically, we show that the optimal policy can consist of carefully randomizing among all arms. Such a mixture policy yields a PM that strictly dominates those yielded by each of the arms individually. The following lemma Lemma 1 (Gini Deviation) Consider a two-arm Bernoulli bandit model. For a given p ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] p\in[0,1] , the arms’ distributions are Bern ⁡ ( p ) {\rm Bern}(p) and Bern ⁡ ( 1 − p ) {\rm Bern}(1-p) , with CDFs 𝔽 1 \mathbb{F}_{1} and F 2 F_{2} , respectively. For distortion function h ⁡ ( u ) = u ⁡ ( 1 − u ) h(u)=u(1-u) , we have U h ​ ( 1 2 ​ 𝔽 1 + 1 2 ​ 𝔽 2 ) &gt; max ⁡ { U h ​ ( 𝔽 1 ) , U h ​ ( 𝔽 2 ) } . \displaystyle U_{h}\left(\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{F}_{1}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{F}_{2}\right)&gt;\max\big\{U_{h}(\mathbb{F}_{1})\;,\;U_{h}(\mathbb{F}_{2})\big\}\ . (3) Proof: See Appendix A . This result emphasizes the important observation that the optimal value of the desired PM is achieved when the samples are generated by the mixture distribution 1 2 ​ ( 𝔽 1 + 𝔽 2 ) \frac{1}{2}(\mathbb{F}_{1}+\mathbb{F}_{2}) . Operationally, this means that to achieve the optimal performance, an MAB algorithm should randomize sampling from the two arms. This work is the first to establish the need for randomized sampling to achieve a desired metric. Throughout the rest of the paper, when the optimal performance is achieved by selecting a mixture of arms, we refer to that as a mixture policy. Otherwise, when it follows the convention of one arm dominating others, we refer to that as a solitary arm policy. Whether the optimal policy is a mixture or solitary, critically hinges on the choice of the distortion function h h , and whether a PM might involve mixtures depends on the distortion function . Categorically, we establish that under convex distortion functions, the optimal solution is always a solitary arm. On the other hand, for any strictly concave distortion function, there is at least one bandit model for which the optimal solution is a mixture policy. Technical Challenge of Learning Optimal Mixtures. Designing an MAB algorithm that can randomize sampling from different arms according to the optimal randomization probabilities is fundamentally from the algorithms with solitary arm policies. Specifically, searching for a solitary arm is guided by sequentially identifying and selecting an arm that optimizes a desired metric (e.g., upper confidence bound). In contrast, a mixture policy introduces two new challenges to the arm-selection policy. • Estimating mixing coefficients: In a K K -arm MAB, the optimal mixture will be specified by K K continuous-valued mixing coefficients. Determining these coefficients inevitably necessitates incorporating routines to reliably estimate them to the desired levels of accuracy. Such estimation routines generally have no precedence in designing bandit algorithms, which generally either lack estimation routines or, even when they do, have auxiliary natures in support of the terminal decision (isolating one arm). Since the optimal set of mixing coefficients lies in a continuous space with infinite possibilities, the nature of mixture policies becomes fundamentally distinct from the solitary arm policies with a decision space with finite possibilities (arms). • Tracking the optimal mixture: The second dimension is the need for an efficient algorithm to track the optimal mixture distribution. This necessitates balancing arm selections over time so that their mixture, in aggregate, conforms to the optimal one. 1.1 Literature Review There are two main lines of research that have considered bandit objectives beyond optimizing the first moment. Empirical Distribution Performance Measures Framework. In the MAB literature, the work most closely related to ours is the empirical distribution performance measure (EDPM) framework [ 14 ] . EDPM shifts focus from mean - based bandits to a richer family of path - dependent objectives which depend on the entire sequence of observed rewards rather than just their average. This framework is primarily motivated by risk criteria such as the Sharpe ratio, value - at - risk (VaR), and conditional value - at - risk (CVaR). These metrics highlight distributional features like variance and tail losses. Optimizing such measures results in having oracles that may change arms over time, unlike the fixed - arm oracle in the conventional setting. This important observation made by [ 14 ] precisely aligns with our key observation that PM-centric optimality policies do not necessarily commit to a single arm and might have mixture structures. Recognizing such a shift, the EDPM framework designs an algorithm and specifies systematic conditions under which a solitary arm policy can approximate a mixture policy. When the path-dependent objective is (quasi-)convex in the space of arms’ distributions, expectedly, the approximate policy becomes exact. Our PM-centric framework has the following major distinctions from the EDPM framework. Objective: We shift away from having approximate solitary policies to characterizing the exact policies. To this end, the emphasis will be on concave settings, for which the optimal policies are unknown — and we show that they do not necessarily admit solitary policies. Assumptions &amp; Scope: There are subtle differences in assumptions on the continuity model of the performance/preference metrics that have important implications on the scope of the bandit settings. Specifically, the EDPM assumes Lipschitz continuity for its performance metric, which excludes important classes of objectives, such as distortion risk measures (DRMs). In contrast, the PM-centric framework assumes only Hölder continuity, accommodating a broader class of objectives, including DRMs. Mixture algorithm: We offer algorithms that implement mixture policies, shifting away from the conventional solitary policies. The PM-centric algorithms are designed to determine and track the optimal mixture of arm selections. Regret guarantees: For the measures such as mean and CVaR, we match their regret bounds using the ETC-based algorithm under the condition that the sub-optimality gap used in the ETC-based algorithm can be expressed as a function of the discretization level. While they have the assumption of boundedness, we consider sub-Gaussian bandit models. However, the ETC-based algorithm assumes the gap knowledge, while their algorithm is UCB-based. Risk-sensitive Bandits. There is a rich line of research on risk sensitivity in MABs. Unlike the canonical objective of maximizing expected reward, risk-sensitive approaches consider finer distributional properties that capture deviation and tail behavior. Widely used risk measures include variance, VaR, CVaR, and quantile-based criteria. Some recent developments on various notations of risk-sensitivity include [ 15 , 16 , 17 , 18 , 19 , 20 ] . In Section 7 , we discuss the connection of our PM-centric framework to the existing literature on risk-sensitive bandits. Furthermore, there are general frameworks for viewing and analyzing risk-sensitivity in decision-making, such as the cumulative prospect theory (CPT) [ 21 ] and the framework based on DRMs [ 22 ] . The study in [ 23 ] introduces a framework that unifies various MAB risk measures, CPT-inspired bandits, and DRM-based bandits. It specified a Wasserstein distance-based approach to delineate concentration bounds for estimating empirical risk and incorporating them in decision-making. Our PM-centric approach has the following major differences from the existing studies on risk-sensitive bandits. Generality: Our approach recovers the existing literature on risk-sensitive bandits as special cases. In particular, when the distortion function is chosen appropriately, the PM-based framework subsumes the DRMs. Objective: The risk-sensitive bandits literature focuses primarily on minimizing different notions of decision variation. Although such an approach penalizes dispersion to avoid tail risk, in some domains we explicitly require emphasizing and valuing unpredictability or convex payoffs in volatility. Designing bandit objectives that reward high variance, enabling favoring variance without forcing an unnatural mean - maximization surrogate. Some common examples include volatility trading, in which a trader buys an at - the - money straddle on an index and earns profit proportional to the absolute price movement. In this case, large swings in either direction are desirable. Another example is wildlife protection or airport security patrols, for which predictability is a weakness. In these examples, an adversary exploits regular patterns. As a counter-measure, the patrols want to monitor routes whose occurrence probabilities are spread out, i.e., have high entropy or variance over actions, to keep the adversary uncertain. Mixture algorithm: The existing literature including the works [ 15 , 16 , 17 , 18 , 19 ] assumes the existence of an optimal solitary arm. We show that for PMs with convex distortion functions, the optimal solution is a solitary arm. In contrast, when the PMs have strictly concave functions, the optimal solution might be a mixture. Our analysis does not rely on any assumptions about the nature of the optimal solution. As a result, our framework not only recovers settings in which the optimal solution is a solitary arm, but also generalizes settings where there is mixture optimality. Regret guarantees: Our general regret guarantees either recover some of the existing regret guarantees (e.g., CVaR) or improve the known guarantees (e.g., proportional hazard transform). 1.2 Contributions We propose a PM-centric framework the objective of which is designing a sequence of arm selections such that a notion of preference — beyond the convention of the expected values of arms’ distributions — is maximized. Notably, in this PM-centric framework, an arm’s utility may no longer be characterized by its mean value. Rather, the quality of an arm is measured by functionals that depend on the entire arm distribution. This framework is highly versatile where by properly selecting the PM and the associated distortion functions, as its special cases, it subsumes the conventional mean-based bandits, the EDPM framework, and the existing approaches to risk-sensitive bandits. In this paper, we address the following overarching questions: What are the key challenges introduced by the PM-centric framework for stochastic bandits? Can we design regret-efficient algorithms for stochastic bandits for optimizing PMs? In pursuing the above question, we uncover a novel and important property of PMs: for many PMs, the optimal utility is achieved not by one arm’s distribution, but a distribution that is the mixture of multiple arms’ distributions. The important implication of this observation is that an algorithm for achieving the optimal regret will not converge to identifying one arm as the best one, and instead converges to a policy that selects a group of arms and selects each overtime according to a very specific frequency. This is a significant departure from the canonical bandit framework, which inherently assumes the existence of a solitary optimal arm. This shift is consequential — most existing algorithms for regret minimization in stochastic bandits are fundamentally designed around the tacit premise that the optimal performance achieved by an oracle is a solitary arm policy. As a result, these algorithms fail to adapt to the PM-centric setting and incur linear regret when applied to settings with optimal mixture policies. In what follows, we discuss the fundamental challenges posed by the PM-centric framework, the limitations of adapting state-of-the-art approaches, and the key technical obstacles in designing and analyzing regret-efficient algorithms under this generalized framework. PM-centric Bandit Algorithms. We begin by highlighting the new design mechanisms that need to be incorporated to transcend from selecting solitary arms and accommodate for learning mixtures over arms. Subsequently, we provide brief overviews of the proposed algorithms for the finite-horizon and infinite-horizon (anytime) settings. Finally, we discuss the technical challenges associated with regret analysis that do not have precedence in the bandit literature. PM-centric Horizon-dependent Algorithms. We first consider the class of finite-horizon algorithms, in which the horizon is known to the learner. This equips the learner with a fine-grained control over constructing confidence sequences to measure the estimation fidelity, such that undesirable error events can be controlled as a decreasing function of the horizon. We provide adaptations of the explore-then-commit (ETC) and upper-confidence bound (UCB) algorithms in the PM-centric framework. These algorithms have major departures from their canonical counterparts — the most significant distinction pertaining to estimating and tracking mixture distributions instead of individual arm distributions. PM-centric Anytime Algorithm. In a wide range of applications, decisions are not bound by fixed horizons (e.g., recommendation systems). Based on UCB principles, we propose an anytime algorithm in Section 5 , in which removing the dependence on budget creates the following technical challenge. A critical step in PM-centric algorithms is a routine for estimating the coefficients needed for mixing the arms’ distributions to generate the optimal mixture. In the horizon-dependent algorithms, the learner can choose the accuracy level for mixing coefficients estimates as a function of the horizon, ensuring tight regret bounds. Intuitively, such accuracy levels are inversely related to the horizon. In the anytime algorithm, specifying the desired estimation accuracy faces the challenge that the horizon is unknown. In our anytime algorithm design in Section 5 , we let the learner set the needed accuracy for mixing coefficient estimates is refined successively as more data is collected. The remainder of this paper is organized as follows. Section 2 introduces the PM-centric bandit framework, along with the necessary definitions, notation, and assumptions. In Section 3 , we present horizon-dependent algorithms and discuss how they differ from their canonical counterparts. Section 4 characterizes regret bounds for these horizon-aware methods. Section 5 then introduces an anytime variant and establishes its regret guarantees. We present empirical evaluations of all proposed algorithms in Section 6 . Finally, Section 7 discusses the application of our framework to risk-sensitive bandits and the relevance of our results to the existing literature. 2 PM-centric Bandit Framework Bandit Model. Consider a K K -armed unstructured stochastic bandit. Each arm i ∈ [ K ] ≜ { 1 , ⋯ , K } i\in[K]\triangleq\{1,\cdots,K\} is endowed with a probability space ( Ω , ℱ , 𝔽 i ) (\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F}_{i}) , where ℱ \mathcal{F} is the σ \sigma -algebra on Ω ⊆ ℝ + \Omega\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}_{+} and 𝔽 i \mathbb{F}_{i} is an unknown probability measure. Oracle Policy. Specifying an oracle that serves as a benchmark for policy performance. Such an oracle accurately identifies the optimal sequence of arm selections { A t : t ∈ ℕ } \{A_{t}:t\in{\mathbb{N}}\} . (7) Mixture-centric Regret. Motivated by the observation that for a given distortion function h h , the optimal sampling rule may be achieved by a mixture of arm distributions, we provide a general mixture-centric framework in which bandit policies can take mixture forms, subsuming the solitary policies as a special case when the mixing mass is placed on one arm. By the oracle policy’s definition 𝜶 𝝂 ⋆ \boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\star}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}} in ( 6 ), for a bandit instance 𝝂 \boldsymbol{\nu} , our objective is to design a bandit algorithm with minimal regret with respect to the PM achieved by the oracle policy. Hence, for a given policy π \pi and horizon T T , we define the regret as the gap between the PM achieved by the oracle policy and the average PM achieved by π \pi , i.e., ℜ 𝝂 π ​ ( T ) ≜ V ⁡ ( 𝜶 ⋆ , 𝔽 ) − 𝔼 𝝂 π ​ [ V ⁡ ( 1 T ​ 𝝉 T π , 𝔽 ) ] . \displaystyle\mathfrak{R}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}^{\pi}(T)\;\triangleq\;V(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\star},\mathbb{F})-{\mathbb{E}}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}^{\pi}\Big[V\Big(\frac{1}{T}\boldsymbol{\tau}_{T}^{\pi},\mathbb{F}\Big)\Big]\ . Next, define Ξ \Xi as the convex hull of the set { 𝔽 i : i ∈ [ K ] } \{\mathbb{F}_{i}:i\in[K]\} and assume U h U_{h} satisfies the following notions of continuity. Definition 1 (Hölder continuity) The PM U h U_{h} is said to be Hölder continuous with exponent q ∈ ( 0 , 1 ] q\in(0,1] , if for all distributions 𝔾 1 , 𝔾 2 ∈ ( Ξ , W 1 ) \mathbb{G}_{1},\mathbb{G}_{2}\in(\Xi,W_{1}) , there exists a finite ℒ ∈ ℝ + \mathcal{L}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+} such that U h ​ ( 𝔾 1 ) − U h ​ ( 𝔾 2 ) ≤ ℒ ​ ‖ 𝔾 1 − 𝔾 2 ‖ W q . \displaystyle U_{h}(\mathbb{G}_{1})-U_{h}(\mathbb{G}_{2})\;\leq\;\mathcal{L}\left\lVert\mathbb{G}_{1}-\mathbb{G}_{2}\right\rVert_{\rm W}^{q}. (10) Many of the widely-used PMs are Hölder-continuous. In Table 3 , we present a list of PMs, and specify their associated Hölder exponents q q . Lemma 2 (Finiteness of U h U_{h} ) Suppose h h is Hölder-continuous. Then, U h ​ ( 𝔾 ) U_{h}(\mathbb{G}) is finite for any 𝔾 ∈ ( Ξ , W 1 ) \mathbb{G}\in(\Xi,W_{1}) . Proof: See Appendix B . For a given Hölder-continuous function h h , we denote the maximum value of the associated PM by B h ∈ ℝ + B_{h}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+} . 3 PM-Centric horizon-dependent Algorithm Design We begin by analyzing the horizon-dependent setting in which the learner knows the fixed horizon and leverages that for designing algorithms. Two fundamental bottlenecks make canonical algorithms inapplicable in the PM-centric setting. • Estimating mixing coefficients versus arm means: Canonical algorithms, such as ETC- and UCB-based algorithms, form estimates of the arm means. Based on the confidence sets constructed around these estimates, these algorithms have various arm selection routines for balancing exploration and exploitation. Since the PM depends on the entire description of the arms’ CDFs (not just their moments), the learner needs to estimate these CDFs up to a desired accuracy to be able to balance exploration and exploitation. Subsequently, we face a functional estimation to determine the mixing coefficients as a function of the estimated arms’ CDFs. Both of these steps have clear distinctions that make the canonical algorithms inapplicable in the mixture-centric setting. • Tracking mixtures: Once the optimal mixtures are estimated, there needs to be a mechanism that translates the mixing coefficient estimates into arm selection decisions. These decisions have to balance two concurrent objectives. First, there is a need to improve the estimates of the mixing coefficients. This may be achieved by exploring the arms to ensure accurate estimates of the CDFs, albeit at the cost of increased regret. Secondly, the arm selection rules need to ensure that, in aggregate, their selections track the mixing estimates. This necessitates an explicit mixture tracking rule that is regret-efficient. In this section, we design algorithms in the PM-centric framework that are regret-efficient. We observe that adaptations of the canonical ETC and UCB algorithms can lead to sublinear regret in the PM-centric setting, each having its own performance or viability (model information) advantages. To start, we provide a few definitions and notations that will be used to describe and analyze the algorithms. We use the shorthand π ∈ { E , U , C } \pi\in\{{\rm E,U,C}\} to refer to the ETC-type, UCB-type, and a computationally efficient UCB-type policies, respectively. An important routine in these algorithms is estimating the arms’ CDFs accurately. We denote the empirical estimate of 𝔽 i \mathbb{F}_{i} at time t t generated by policy π \pi by 𝔽 i , t π ( x ) ≜ 1 τ t π ​ ( i ) ∑ s ∈ [ t ] : A s = i 𝟙 { X s ≤ x } . \displaystyle\mathbb{F}_{i,t}^{\pi}(x)\triangleq\frac{1}{\tau_{t}^{\pi}(i)}\sum\limits_{s\in[t]:A_{s}=i}\mathds{1}\left\{X_{s}\leq x\right\}\ . (11) Knowing the horizon equips the learner with the following key advantage. Analyzing the accuracy of these estimates and their impacts on the arm-selection decisions involves constructing confidence sets that measure the uncertainty of the CDF (and PM) estimates. In designing horizon-dependent confidence sets, the learner has a fine-grained control over undesirable error events in CDF estimation measured in terms of the horizon T T . This leads to stronger performance guarantees compared to the settings in which the horizon information is unavailable. To analyze and control the undesirable error events in CDF estimates, we leverage the following lemma, which provides a concentration bound on these empirical CDFs in the 1 1 -Wasserstein metric. Lemma 3 For any policy π \pi and any y ∈ ℝ + y\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+} we have ℙ 𝝂 π \displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}^{\pi} ( ‖ 𝔽 i , t π − 𝔽 i ‖ W &gt; y ) ≤ 2 ​ exp ⁡ ( − τ t π ​ ( i ) 256 ​ e ​ ( y − 512 τ t π ​ ( i ) ) 2 ) . \displaystyle\Big(\left\lVert\mathbb{F}_{i,t}^{\pi}-\mathbb{F}_{i}\right\rVert_{\rm W}&gt;y\Big)\leq 2\exp\Bigg(-\;\frac{\tau^{\pi}_{t}(i)}{256{\rm e}}\Big(y-\frac{512}{\sqrt{\tau^{\pi}_{t}(i)}}\Big)^{2}\Bigg)\ . (12) Proof: See [ 23 , Lemma 8] . Using ( 12 ), we can construct distribution confidence sets to have a horizon-dependent control over error events. Specifically, choosing y y such that the right-hand side of ( 12 ) falls below 1 T 2 \frac{1}{T^{2}} ensures that aggregate error events accumulate in a regret which is sublinear in T T . Discretizing Mixing Coefficients. Given a finite number of samples, the fidelity of the estimates of 𝜶 ⋆ \boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\star} cannot be made arbitrarily high (information-theoretic limits). Furthermore, even if the exact 𝜶 ⋆ \boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\star} is known, they can generally take arbitrary irrational values while sampling frequencies are always rational values. Hence, estimating 𝜶 ⋆ \boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\star} beyond a certain accuracy level is not only infeasible, but also is not beneficial. We define ε \varepsilon to specify the desired level of fidelity for each mixing coefficient. Accordingly, we specify Δ ε K − 1 \Delta^{K-1}_{\varepsilon} by uniformly discretizing each coordinate of Δ K − 1 \Delta^{K-1} into intervals of length ε \varepsilon (when 1 ε \frac{1}{\varepsilon} is not an integer, we make up for the deficit/excess of the weights in the last coordinate). The precise choices of discretization will be slightly different in the ETC and UBC algorithms and will be specified in Sections 3.1 and 3.2 , respectively. For a given discretized simplex Δ ε K − 1 \Delta^{K-1}_{\varepsilon} , we define the top three discrete mixing coefficients chosen from Δ ε K − 1 \Delta^{K-1}_{\varepsilon} as 𝐚 ( 1 ) ≜ arg ​ max 𝐚 ∈ Δ ε K − 1 V ( 𝐚 , 𝔽 ) , 𝐚 ( 2 ) ≜ arg ​ max 𝐚 ∈ Δ ε K − 1 : 𝐚 ( 2 ) ≠ 𝐚 ( 1 ) V ( 𝐚 , 𝔽 ) . \displaystyle\mathbf{a}^{(1)}\triangleq\argmax\limits_{\mathbf{a}\in\Delta^{K-1}_{\varepsilon}}\;V(\mathbf{a},\mathbb{F})\ ,\quad\mathbf{a}^{(2)}\triangleq\argmax\limits_{\mathbf{a}\in\Delta^{K-1}_{\varepsilon}:\mathbf{a}^{(2)}\neq\mathbf{a}^{(1)}}\;V(\mathbf{a},\mathbb{F})\ . (13) We also adopt the convention 𝐚 ( 0 ) ≜ 𝜶 ⋆ \mathbf{a}^{(0)}\triangleq\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\star} . Furthermore, we introduce three suboptimality gap terms within the space of the discrete simplex Δ ε K − 1 \Delta_{\varepsilon}^{K-1} . The suboptimality gaps play important roles in designing the ETC-based algorithm and in analyzing both the ETC-based and UCB-based algorithms. Let us define δ i ​ j ​ ( ε ) \displaystyle\delta_{ij}(\varepsilon) ≜ V ⁡ ( 𝐚 ( i ) , 𝔽 ) − V ⁡ ( 𝐚 ( j ) , 𝔽 ) , ∀ i , j ∈ { 0 , 1 , 2 } . \displaystyle\triangleq V\Big(\mathbf{a}^{(i)},\mathbb{F}\Big)-V\Big(\mathbf{a}^{(j)},\mathbb{F}\Big)\ ,\qquad\forall i,j\in\{0,1,2\}\ . (14) These gap terms appear in different forms in the performance (regret) guarantees. 3.1 PM-centric ETC for Mixtures We propose the PM -centric ETC for M ixtures (PM-ETC-M) algorithm, following the ETC principles, albeit with important modifications needed to accommodate implementing mixture policies. Conventional ETC. The conventional ETC algorithm implements two phases within a known horizon T T . In the first phase (exploration phase), ETC samples arms uniformly at random to estimate the arms’ means. At the end of the exploration phase, ETC identifies the arm that is expected to have the largest mean and commits to this arm for the remainder of the horizon. An adaptation of ETC to the PM-centric framework leads to two challenges. First, at the end of the exploration phase, we face an infinite-dimensional problem (owing to the continuous space Δ K − 1 \Delta^{K-1} ), as opposed to a finite-dimensional problem of identifying the arm with the largest mean. Secondly, at the end of the exploration phase, there is no single arm that we can commit to. Rather, we need to balance arm selection proportions to ensure a regret-efficient allotment of resources based on the estimated optimal mixing coefficient from the exploration phase. Adapting ETC for PMs. The PM-ETC-M algorithm consists of an initial exploration phase during which the arms are sampled uniformly for a fixed interval to form high-fidelity estimates of the arms’ CDFs (in contrast to canonical ETC, which estimates arms’ mean values). Similarly to ETC, the duration of this phase depends on the minimum sub-optimality gap. Subsequently, in the next phase, the ETC algorithm commits to a fixed policy, which is a mixture of arms with pre-fixed mixing coefficients. To alleviate the issue of infinite dimensionality of the optimization space, we adopt the discrete simplex Δ ε K − 1 \Delta_{\varepsilon}^{K-1} instead of Δ K − 1 \Delta^{K-1} , which preserves the finite-dimensional setting of ETC at the end of the exploration phase. For the ETC algorithm, we set the discrete simplex as follows. Δ ε K − 1 ≜ { ε 𝐧 : 𝐧 ∈ ℕ K and 𝟏 ⊤ ⋅ ε 𝐧 = 1 } , \displaystyle\Delta^{K-1}_{\varepsilon}\triangleq\{\varepsilon\mathbf{n}:\mathbf{n}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{K}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathbf{1}^{\top}\cdot\varepsilon\mathbf{n}=1\}\ , (15) where 𝟏 \mathbf{1} denotes the K K -dimensional all-one vector. The ETC algorithm is designed to select 𝐚 ( 1 ) \mathbf{a}^{(1)} as the mixing coefficient with high probability. The main processes of this algorithm are explained next, and its pseudocode is presented in Algorithm 1 . Algorithm 1 PM-ETC-M 1: Input: Suboptimality gap δ 12 ​ ( ε ) \delta_{12}(\varepsilon) , horizon T T 2: Sample each arm ⌈ N ⁡ ( ε ) / K ⌉ \lceil N(\varepsilon)/K\rceil times and obtain observation sequences 𝒳 ⌈ N ⁡ ( ε ) / K ⌉ ​ ( 1 ) , ⋯ , 𝒳 ⌈ N ⁡ ( ε ) / K ⌉ ​ ( K ) \mathcal{X}_{\lceil N(\varepsilon)/K\rceil}(1),\cdots,\mathcal{X}_{\lceil N(\varepsilon)/K\rceil}(K) 3: Initialize: τ K E ​ ( i ) = ⌈ N ⁡ ( ε ) / K ⌉ ; ∀ i ∈ [ K ] \tau_{K}^{\rm E}(i)=\lceil N(\varepsilon)/K\rceil;\forall\;i\in[K] , empirical arm CDFs 𝔽 1 , ⌈ N ⁡ ( ε ) / K ⌉ E , ⋯ , 𝔽 K , ⌈ N ⁡ ( ε ) / K ⌉ E \mathbb{F}^{\rm E}_{1,\lceil N(\varepsilon)/K\rceil},\cdots,\mathbb{F}^{\rm E}_{K,\lceil N(\varepsilon)/K\rceil} 4: for t = K ⁡ ⌈ N ⁡ ( ε ) / K ⌉ + 1 , ⋯ , T t=K\lceil N(\varepsilon)/K\rceil+1,\cdots,T do 5: Select an arm A t A_{t} via ( 3.1 ) and obtain reward X t X_{t} 6: Update the empirical CDF 𝔽 A t , t E \mathbb{F}^{\rm E}_{A_{t},t} according to ( 11 ) 7: end for Explore (Estimate Mixing coefficients). The purpose of this phase is to form high-confidence estimates of the empirical arm CDFs. We specify a time instant N ⁡ ( ε ) N(\varepsilon) that determines the duration of the exploration phase, and that is the instance by which we have confident enough CDF estimates. The arms are selected uniformly, each ⌈ 1 K ​ N ​ ( ε ) ⌉ \lceil\frac{1}{K}N(\varepsilon)\rceil times. For a PM with a distortion function that has Hölder continuity exponent q q and constant ℒ \mathcal{L} , the time instant N ⁡ ( ε ) N(\varepsilon) becomes a function of q q , ℒ \mathcal{L} , the horizon T T , and the minimum sub-optimality gap δ 12 ​ ( ε ) \delta_{12}(\varepsilon) as specified below. N ⁡ ( ε ) ≜ 256 ​ K ​ e ​ ( 2 ​ K ​ ℒ δ 12 ​ ( ε ) ) 2 q ​ [ 32 e + log 1 2 ⁡ ( 2 ​ K ​ T 2 ​ ( ε − ( K − 1 ) + 1 ) ) ] 2 . \displaystyle N(\varepsilon)\triangleq 256K{\rm e}\left(\frac{2K\mathcal{L}}{\delta_{12}(\varepsilon)}\right)^{\frac{2}{q}}\bigg[\frac{32}{\sqrt{\rm e}}+\log^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big(2KT^{2}\big(\varepsilon^{-(K-1)}+1\big)\Big)\bigg]^{2}\ . (16) Note that N ⁡ ( ε ) N(\varepsilon) scales with T T according to O ⁡ ( log ⁡ T ) O(\log T) . Accordingly we define M ⁡ ( ε ) ≜ N ⁡ ( ε ) log ⁡ T , \displaystyle M(\varepsilon)\triangleq\frac{N(\varepsilon)}{\log T}\ , (17) which based on ( 16 ) scales as O ⁡ ( 1 ) O(1) . Next, using the collected samples during exploration, the PM-ETC-M algorithm constructs the empirical estimates of arms’ CDFs, as specified in ( 11 ). Choosing the Mixing Coefficient. At the time instant N ⁡ ( ε ) N(\varepsilon) , the PM-ETC-M algorithm identifies the discrete mixture coefficients that maximize the PM using the empirical CDFs. These coefficients are denoted by 𝐚 N ⁡ ( ε ) E \mathbf{a}^{\rm E}_{N(\varepsilon)} , where we have defined 𝐚 t E ∈ arg ​ max 𝐚 ∈ Δ ε K − 1 ⁡ U h ​ ( ∑ i ∈ [ K ] a ⁡ ( i ) ​ 𝔽 i , t E ) . \displaystyle\mathbf{a}^{\rm E}_{t}\;\in\;\argmax\limits_{\mathbf{a}\in\Delta^{K-1}_{\varepsilon}}U_{h}\Bigg(\sum\limits_{i\in[K]}a(i)\;\mathbb{F}_{i,t}^{\rm E}\Bigg)\ . (18) Tracking Mixtures (Commit). For the remaining sampling instants t ∈ [ N ⁡ ( ε ) , T ] t\in[N(\varepsilon),T] , the PM-ETC-M algorithm commits to selecting the arms such that their selection frequencies are as close as possible to 𝐚 N ⁡ ( ε ) E \mathbf{a}^{\rm E}_{N(\varepsilon)} . Until the time instant N ⁡ ( ε ) N(\varepsilon) , the algorithm samples all arms uniformly. Uniform sampling results in some arms being sampled more than what the policy 𝐚 N ⁡ ( ε ) E \mathbf{a}^{\rm E}_{N(\varepsilon)} dictates and hence, these arms will not be sampled again. Having over-sampled arms implies that some arms are under-sampled. In that case, these arms would be sampled such that the number of times they are chosen converges to the mixing coefficient 𝐚 N ⁡ ( ε ) E \mathbf{a}^{\rm E}_{N(\varepsilon)} . To formalize how to track the mixture, let S S be the set of the first ( K − 1 ) (K-1) arms (or any desired set of arms). For each arm i ∈ S i\in S , the algorithm calculates the required number of samples, which is T ​ a t ​ ( i ) Ta_{t}(i) for t = N ⁡ ( ε ) t={N(\varepsilon)} and horizon T T . The sampling procedure proceeds as follows: 1. if T ​ a t ​ ( i ) &gt; ⌈ t K ⌉ Ta_{t}(i)&gt;\lceil\frac{t}{K}\rceil at t = N ⁡ ( ε ) t=N(\varepsilon) , we interpret that arm i i is not explored sufficiently, and we proceed with sampling arm i i to T ​ a N ⁡ ( ε ) ​ ( i ) Ta_{N(\varepsilon)}(i) before moving on to the next arm. 2. if T ​ a t ​ ( i ) ≤ ⌈ t K ⌉ Ta_{t}(i)\leq\lceil\frac{t}{K}\rceil at t = N ⁡ ( ε ) t=N(\varepsilon) , we interpret that arm i i is explored sufficiently and we skip selecting it. After we apply these conditions on the first ( K − 1 ) (K-1) arms, we allocate the remaining sampling budget to arm K K . Discussion. An important advantage of the PM-ETC-M algorithm is its computational simplicity. The algorithm involves uniform exploration for a finite interval, followed by committing to a mixture estimate based on the data in the exploration phase. UCB-type algorithms, on the other hand, require substantially larger computation due to optimizing over confidence sets. Hence, in scenarios with a computational complexity bottleneck, PM-ETC-M serves as a regret-efficient algorithm to optimize PMs in an online setting. Furthermore, even if we have access to sufficient computational resources, PM-ETC-M may potentially have better regret guarantees depending on the choice of the PM. In Section 3.2 , we will discuss that the relative performances of PM-ETC-M versus that of the UCB-based counterpart depends on the choice of PM, and neither has a uniform regret advantage over the other. It is also noteworthy that despite its computational simplicity and its better regret guarantee for some PMs, PM-ETC-M inherits the crucial drawback of ETC-type algorithms — the knowledge of instance-dependent gap information. We observe that the PM-ETC-M algorithm relies on the instance-specific gap information (through N ⁡ ( ε ) N(\varepsilon) ), which may not always be available — an issue that is addressed by the UCB-type algorithms. 3.2 PM-centric UCB for Mixtures A critical bottleneck of the PM-ETC-M algorithm is that its decisions hinge on knowing the instance-dependent gap through the exploration parameter N ⁡ ( ε ) N(\varepsilon) . As in the conventional setting, we adopt a confidence-based approach to address the drawback of PM-ETC-M. We begin by providing a brief overview of the UCB algorithm, detailing the bottlenecks in the direct adaptation of the UCB algorithm in the PM-centric setting. Overview of and Comparison with Canonical UCB. The UCB algorithm forms estimates of the arm means and constructs confidence sets around these estimates, quantifying the estimation fidelity. Contrasted to the ETC algorithm, which balances exploration and exploitation through the instance-dependent N ⁡ ( ε ) N(\varepsilon) , UCB uses the confidence sequences to balance exploration and exploitation in a regret-efficient fashion. Specifically, at each round, UCB computes the upper confidence bound for each arm and samples the one with the largest upper confidence bound. Sampling an arm reduces the uncertainty in its estimate, and it can be shown that no suboptimal arm is sampled more than O ⁡ ( log ⁡ T ) O(\log T) times, resulting in sublinear regret. In the PM-centric setting, we have significant distinctions from the canonical setting. First, as in PM-ETC-M, we estimate the entire CDFs of the arms instead of their first moments. Secondly, upper confidence bounds for each arm do not directly apply in the PM-centric setting. Instead, we need to translate distribution confidence sets to confidence bounds for discrete mixing coefficients in the (discrete) simplex for estimating mixtures. Third, we need an additional routine to translate the estimated mixing coefficients to arm selection decisions. Adapting UCB for PMs. We now present the PM -centric UCB for M ixtures (PM-UCB-M) algorithm, which does not require the information on the sub-optimality gap N ⁡ ( ε ) N(\varepsilon) . The salient features of this algorithm are (i) a distribution estimation routine for forming high-confidence estimates for arms’ CDFs and subsequently mixture coefficients; and (ii) a sampling rule based on an under-sampling criteria to ensure that arm selections track the mixture coefficients. For the UCB algorithm, we set the discrete simplex as follows. Δ ε K − 1 ≜ { ε 𝐧 : 𝐧 ∈ ℕ K and 𝟏 ⊤ ⋅ ε ( 𝐧 + 1 2 ) = 1 } . \displaystyle\Delta^{K-1}_{\varepsilon}\triangleq\left\{\varepsilon\mathbf{n}:\mathbf{n}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{K}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathbf{1}^{\top}\cdot\varepsilon\Big(\mathbf{n}+\frac{1}{2}\Big)=1\right\}\ . (19) The pseudocode of PM-UCB-M is presented in Algorithm 2 . Algorithm 2 PM-UCB-M 1: Input: Exploration rate ρ \rho , horizon T T 2: Sample each arm N ⁡ ( ρ , ε ) ≜ ⌈ ρ ​ T ​ ε / 4 ⌉ N(\rho,\varepsilon)\triangleq\lceil\rho T\varepsilon/4\rceil times and obtain observation sequences 𝒳 K ​ N ​ ( ρ , ε ) ​ ( 1 ) , ⋯ , 𝒳 K ​ N ​ ( ρ , ε ) ​ ( K ) \mathcal{X}_{KN(\rho,\varepsilon)}(1),\cdots,\mathcal{X}_{KN(\rho,\varepsilon)}(K) 3: Initialize: τ K ​ N ​ ( ρ , ε ) U ​ ( i ) = N ⁡ ( ρ , ε ) \tau_{KN(\rho,\varepsilon)}^{\rm U}(i)=N(\rho,\varepsilon) ∀ i ∈ [ K ] \;\forall i\in[K] , emprical arm CDFs 𝔽 1 , K ​ N ​ ( ρ , ε ) U , ⋯ , 𝔽 K , K ​ N ​ ( ρ , ε ) U \mathbb{F}^{\rm U}_{1,KN(\rho,\varepsilon)},\cdots,\mathbb{F}^{\rm U}_{K,KN(\rho,\varepsilon)} , confidence sets 𝒞 K ​ N ​ ( ρ , ε ) ( 1 ) , ⋯ 𝒞 K ​ N ​ ( ρ , ε ) ( K ) \mathcal{C}_{KN(\rho,\varepsilon)}(1),\cdots\mathcal{C}_{KN(\rho,\varepsilon)}(K) according to ( 20 ) 4: for t = K ​ N ​ ( ρ , ε ) + 1 , ⋯ , T t=KN(\rho,\varepsilon)+1,\cdots,T do 5: Select an arm A t A_{t} via ( 22 ) and obtain reward X t X_{t} 6: Update the empirical CDF 𝔽 A t , t U \mathbb{F}^{\rm U}_{A_{t},t} according to ( 11 ) 7: Update the confidence set 𝒞 t ​ ( A t ) \mathcal{C}_{t}(A_{t}) according to ( 20 ) 8: Compute the optimistic estimate 𝐚 t U \mathbf{a}_{t}^{\rm U} according to ( 21 ) 9: end for Choosing the Mixing Coefficient. Based on the concentration of CDF estimates ( 12 ), at time t t and given the empirical CDFs { 𝔽 i , t U : i ∈ [ K ] } \{\mathbb{F}_{i,t}^{\rm U}:i\in[K]\} , for arm i ∈ [ K ] i\in[K] defined in ( 11 ), we define the distribution confidence space as the collection of all the distributions that are within a bounded 1 1 -Wasserstein distance of 𝔽 i , t U \mathbb{F}_{i,t}^{\rm U} . Specifically, for each i ∈ [ K ] i\in[K] , we define 𝒞 t ( i ) ≜ { η ∈ Ω \displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{t}(i)\triangleq\bigg\{\eta\in\Omega : ‖ 𝔽 i , t U − η ‖ W ≤ 16 2 ​ e ​ log ⁡ T + 32 τ t U ​ ( i ) } . \displaystyle:\left\lVert\mathbb{F}_{i,t}^{\rm U}-\eta\right\rVert_{\rm W}\leq 16\ \frac{\sqrt{2{\rm e}\log T}+32}{\sqrt{\tau^{\rm U}_{t}(i)}}\bigg\}\ . (20) Next, we also need to estimate the optimal mixing coefficients. For this purpose, we apply the UCB principle. This, in turn, requires that we compute the following optimistic estimates for the mixing coefficients: 𝐚 t U ∈ arg ​ max 𝐚 ∈ Δ ε K − 1 ⁡ max η i ∈ 𝒞 t ​ ( i ) , ∀ i ∈ [ K ] ​ U h ​ ( ∑ i ∈ [ K ] a ⁡ ( i ) ​ η i ) . \displaystyle\mathbf{a}^{\rm U}_{t}\in\argmax\limits_{\mathbf{a}\in\Delta_{\varepsilon}^{K-1}}\;\max\limits_{\eta_{i}\in\mathcal{C}_{t}(i),\forall i\in[K]}\;U_{h}\Big(\sum\limits_{i\in[K]}a(i)\;\eta_{i}\Big)\ . (21) Tracking Mixtures. Once we have estimates of the optimal mixing coefficients, i.e., 𝐚 t U \mathbf{a}_{t}^{\rm U} , we design an arm selection rule that translates these coefficients and CDF estimates to arm selection choices. Designing such a rule requires addressing the following technical challenges. First, the optimal mixing coefficients might not be unique. Let us denote them by { ψ ℓ : ℓ ∈ [ L ] } \{\psi_{\ell}:\ell\in[L]\} . It is critical to ensure that we consistently track only one of these optimal choices over time. The reason is that if we track multiple mixtures, in aggregate, we will be tracking a mixture of { ψ ℓ : ℓ ∈ [ L ] } \{\psi_{\ell}:\ell\in[L]\} , which is not necessarily optimal. Secondly, in the initial sampling rounds, the estimates 𝜶 t U \boldsymbol{\alpha}_{t}^{\rm U} are relatively inaccurate, and tracking them leads to highly suboptimal decisions. Finally, we need a rule for translating the estimated mixtures to arm selections. Next, we discuss how we address these issues. • Multiple optimal mixtures: Switching among multiple discrete mixtures hinders the tracking mechanism, since we may fail to track any of these optimal coefficients, rather a combination of these which is likely suboptimal. To ensure tracking only one optimal mixture, at time t t , the PM-UCB-M algorithm checks if the coefficient from the previous step, i.e., 𝐚 t − 1 U \mathbf{a}^{\rm U}_{t-1} also maximizes ( 21 ). If it does, then 𝐚 t − 1 U \mathbf{a}^{\rm U}_{t-1} is chosen as a candidate optimistic estimate 𝐚 t U \mathbf{a}^{\rm U}_{t} . Otherwise, any random candidate solving ( 21 ) is chosen. • Initial rounds: To circumvent estimation inaccuracies in the initial rounds, we introduce an explicit exploration phase for each arm. Specifically, for ρ ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \rho\in(0,1) , for the first K ​ ⌈ ρ ​ T ​ ε 4 ⌉ K\lceil\frac{\rho T\varepsilon}{4}\rceil rounds, we explore the arms in a round-robin fashion to initiate the algorithm with sufficiently accurate estimates of the arm CDFs. • Decision rules: When all the arms are sufficiently explored, motivated by the effective approaches to best arm identification [ 24 , 25 , 26 , 27 ] , we sample the most under-sampled arm. An arm is considered under-sampled if it has been sampled less frequently than the rate indicated by the estimate 𝐚 t U \mathbf{a}^{\rm U}_{t} , and has the largest gap between its current fraction and its estimated fraction a t U ​ ( i ) a^{\rm U}_{t}(i) . At time t ≥ K ​ ⌈ ρ ​ T ​ ε 4 ⌉ t\geq K\lceil\frac{\rho T\varepsilon}{4}\rceil , the arm selection rule is specified by A t + 1 ≜ arg ​ max i ∈ [ K ] ⁡ { ta t U ​ ( i ) − τ t U ​ ( i ) } . \displaystyle A_{t+1}\;\triangleq\;\argmax\limits_{i\in[K]}\;\left\{ta^{\rm U}_{t}(i)-\tau_{t}^{\rm U}(i)\right\}\ . (22) 3.3 Computationally Efficient CE-UCB-M In the PM-UCB-M algorithm, determining the mixing coefficients 𝐚 t U \mathbf{a}^{\rm U}_{t} via ( 21 ) involves extremization over a class of distribution functions, and it is computationally expensive. To circumvent this, we present the C omputationally- E fficient PM-centric UCB for M ixtures (CE-UCB-M) algorithm as a computationally tractable modification of PM-UCB-M. In CE-UCB-M, instead of solving ( 21 ), for any given set of CDF estimates { 𝔽 i , t C : i ∈ [ K ] } \{\mathbb{F}_{i,t}^{\rm C}:i\in[K]\} defined in ( 11 ) and mixing vector 𝐚 \mathbf{a} , we define UCB t ​ ( 𝐚 ) \displaystyle{\rm UCB}_{t}(\mathbf{a}) ≜ U h ​ ( ∑ i ∈ [ K ] a ⁡ ( i ) ​ 𝔽 i , t C ) + ℒ ​ ∑ i ∈ [ K ] ( a ⁡ ( i ) ⋅ 16 ​ 2 ​ e ​ log ⁡ T + 32 τ t U ​ ( i ) ) q , \displaystyle\;\triangleq\;U_{h}\left(\sum\limits_{i\in[K]}a(i)\;\mathbb{F}_{i,t}^{\rm C}\right)+\mathcal{L}\sum\limits_{i\in[K]}\bigg(a(i)\cdot 16\;\frac{\sqrt{2{\rm e}\log T}+32}{\sqrt{\tau^{\rm U}_{t}(i)}}\bigg)^{q}\ , (23) where q q and ℒ \mathcal{L} are the Hölder parameters of the underlying distortion function h h . We specify estimates of the mixing coefficients as 𝐚 t C ∈ arg ​ max 𝐚 ∈ Δ ε K − 1 ⁡ UCB t ​ ( 𝐚 ) . \displaystyle\mathbf{a}_{t}^{\rm C}\;\in\;\argmax\limits_{\mathbf{a}\in\Delta_{\varepsilon}^{K-1}}\;{\rm UCB}_{t}(\mathbf{a})\ . (24) The remainder of the algorithm (the tracking block) follows the same steps as in PM-UCB-M. The CE-UCB-M procedure is summarized in Algorithm 3 . Unlike PM-ETC-M, the PM-UCB-M and the CE-UCB-M algorithms are independent of instance-dependent parameters. The explicit exploration phase involves a hyperparameter ρ \rho , which must be bounded away from 0 0 . The performance of PM-ETC-M and PM-UCB-M depends on the choice of PM, with neither uniformly dominating the other. In Section 4 , we show that under some conditions, PM-ETC-M has better regret guarantees for various PMs. However, there are also PMs, such as PHT, for which PM-UCB-M outperforms PM-ETC-M. 4 Regret Analysis for Horizon-dependent Algorithms In this section, we establish the performance guarantees for the algorithms provided in Section 3 . We begin by stating some properties of PMs and then provide upper bounds on the average regret for the three algorithms in Section 3 . Properties of PMs. In Lemma 1 , we observed that choosing the distortion function h ⁡ ( p ) = p ⁡ ( 1 − p ) h(p)=p(1-p) (corresponding to Gini deviation) leads to an optimal mixture in a two-armed Bernoulli bandit. To make this observation more concrete, we now explore broader classes of bandit instances – identifying conditions under which the optimal solution is a solitary arm versus when it is a mixture . Our first key finding is that when the distortion function is convex , the optimal solution is always solitary . This observation is formalized in the following theorem. Theorem 2 (Convex Distortion Functions) Suppose the distortion function h h is convex and the PMs { U h ​ ( 𝔽 i ) : i ∈ [ K ] } \{U_{h}(\mathbb{F}_{i}):i\in[K]\} are finite. Then, U h U_{h} is maximized by a solitary arm, i.e., for any 𝐚 ∈ Δ K − 1 \mathbf{a}\in\Delta^{K-1} , U h ​ ( ∑ i ∈ [ K ] a ⁡ ( i ) ​ 𝔽 i ) ≤ max i ∈ [ K ] ⁡ U h ​ ( 𝔽 i ) . \displaystyle U_{h}\Bigg(\sum_{i\in[K]}a(i)\mathbb{F}_{i}\Bigg)\leq\max_{i\in[K]}\;U_{h}(\mathbb{F}_{i})\ . (25) Proof: See Appendix D.1 . We note that a similar property has also been shown for quasiconvex functionals in [ 14 ] . Convexity holds for various risk measures studied in [ 14 ] , such as variance, entropic risk, and mean-variance. Under convexity, as a consequence of Theorem 2 , we have the additional information that the optimal arm is solitary. While the algorithms presented in Section 3 work for solitary arms, as we will see shortly, their regret guarantees may be weaker compared to the algorithm presented in [ 14 ] for certain PMs. This is the expense paid for the generality to accommodate mixture policies, too. Next, we show that when the distortion function h h is concave, the optimal policy is not necessarily solitary. Specifically, we show that for any given strictly concave h h , there always exists a bandit instance for which the optimal policy is strictly mixture, i.e., the PM associated with the mixture of the arms’ distributions strictly dominates that of each individual arm. Theorem 3 (Strictly Concave Distortion Functions) Suppose the distortion function h h is strictly concave. Then there exists a K K -armed bandit instance for which a mixture of arms maximizes the induced PM U h U_{h} . Proof: See Appendix D.2 . This observation generalizes the anecdotal example of Gini deviation and Bernoulli bandits discussed in Lemma 1 . It highlights the importance of designing algorithms that can adapt to mixture-optimal scenarios. The specific choice of the bandit model that results in mixture-optimal policies depends on the choice of the PM. For instance, Table 2 shows that for Bernoulli bandits, several performance measures with concave distortion functions, such as mean–median deviation, inter - ES range, Wang’s right - tail deviation, and Gini deviation—still exhibit mixture optimality. Regret Analysis. The performance (regret) of the PM-centric algorithms inevitably depends on the quality of the estimates we find for the mixing coefficients. With a finite number of samples, the estimation errors are guaranteed to be bounded away from zero, rendering the implementation of the mixing coefficients different from their optimal values. Hence, the regret terms can be decoupled into two parts: (i) arm selection regret term, which accounts for the regret incurred due to the suboptimal selection of arms over time. This regret term is non-zero even when the mixing coefficients are known perfectly, and is the counterpart of the regret in the conventional bandit settings; and (ii) estimation regret term that accounts for the noise in estimating mixing coefficients. Owing to the discretization process we have introduced, this term can also be considered the discretization error. Using this decomposition, we first present regret bounds as a function of the discretization granularity ε \varepsilon , which controls how finely the action space is discretized. Subsequently, we also provide an ε \varepsilon -independent regret in which ε \varepsilon is chosen carefully to achieve the (near-)best performance subject to algorithmic constraints. For a given discretization level ε \varepsilon and a bandit instance 𝝂 ≜ ( 𝔽 1 , ⋯ , 𝔽 K ) \boldsymbol{\nu}\triangleq(\mathbb{F}_{1},\cdots,\mathbb{F}_{K}) , for policy π \pi , we decompose the regret defined in ( 8 ) into an estimation regret component and an arm selection regret component as follows. ℜ 𝝂 π ​ ( T ) \displaystyle\mathfrak{R}^{\pi}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}(T)\; = ℜ ¯ 𝝂 , i π ​ ( T ) + δ 0 ​ i ​ ( ε ) , i ∈ { 1 , 2 } , \displaystyle=\;\bar{\mathfrak{R}}_{\boldsymbol{\nu},i}^{\pi}(T)+\delta_{0i}(\varepsilon)\ ,\qquad i\in\{1,2\}\ , (26) where we have defined δ 0 ​ i ​ ( ε ) \delta_{0i}(\varepsilon) as the estimation regret, which is specified as follows based on the definition of 𝐚 ( i ) \mathbf{a}^{(i)} in ( 13 ). δ 0 ​ i ​ ( ε ) \displaystyle\delta_{0i}(\varepsilon) ≜ V ⁡ ( 𝐚 ( 0 ) , 𝔽 ) − V ⁡ ( 𝐚 ( i ) , 𝔽 ) , i ∈ { 1 , 2 } , \displaystyle\triangleq V\Big(\mathbf{a}^{(0)},\mathbb{F}\Big)-V\Big(\mathbf{a}^{(i)},\mathbb{F}\Big)\ ,\qquad i\in\{1,2\}\ , (27) where 𝐚 ( 0 ) ≜ 𝜶 ⋆ \mathbf{a}^{(0)}\triangleq\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\star} . We have also defined ℜ ¯ 𝝂 , i π ​ ( T ) \bar{\mathfrak{R}}_{\boldsymbol{\nu},i}^{\pi}(T) as the arm selection regret term, which is specified as follows using the definitions in ( 8 ) and ( 27 ). ℜ ¯ 𝝂 , i π ​ ( T ) \displaystyle\bar{\mathfrak{R}}_{\boldsymbol{\nu},i}^{\pi}(T) ≜ ℜ 𝝂 π ​ ( T ) − δ 0 ​ i ​ ( ε ) = V ⁡ ( 𝐚 ( i ) , 𝔽 ) − 𝔼 𝝂 π ​ [ V ⁡ ( 1 T ​ 𝝉 T π , 𝔽 ) ] , i ∈ { 1 , 2 } . \displaystyle\triangleq\mathfrak{R}^{\pi}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}(T)-\delta_{0i}(\varepsilon)=V\Big(\mathbf{a}^{(i)},\mathbb{F}\Big)-{\mathbb{E}}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}^{\pi}\Big[V\Big(\frac{1}{T}\boldsymbol{\tau}_{T}^{\pi},\mathbb{F}\Big)\Big]\ ,\qquad i\in\{1,2\}\ . (28) Throughout the rest of this section, we provide regret guarantees for all the algorithms. For each algorithm, we provide two guarantees, one as a function of ε \varepsilon and one independent of it. Theorem 4 (PM-ETC-M – ε \varepsilon -dependent) For any ε ∈ ℝ + \varepsilon\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+} and distortion function h h with Hölder exponent q q , for all T &gt; N ⁡ ( ε ) T&gt;N(\varepsilon) , PM-ETC-M’s regret is upper bounded as ℜ 𝝂 E ​ ( T ) \displaystyle\mathfrak{R}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}^{{{\rm E}}}(T)\ ≤ ( ℒ ​ K + W − q ) ​ ( 3 ​ W ​ M ​ ( ε ) ​ log ⁡ T T ) q + δ 01 ​ ( ε ) , \displaystyle\leq(\mathcal{L}K+W^{-q})\bigg(3WM(\varepsilon)\;\frac{\log T}{T}\bigg)^{q}+\delta_{01}(\varepsilon)\ , (29) where M ⁡ ( ε ) M(\varepsilon) , defined in ( 17 ), scales as O ⁡ ( 1 ) O(1) . Proof: See Appendix I.4 . Proof sketch. The proof follows the general principles of ETC with the following differences. (i) Designing the exploration horizon necessitates appropriately bounding error events due to CDF estimation, as opposed to mean estimation in the canonical setting, ultimately ensuring that we estimate the best or the second-best discrete mixing coefficient with a sufficiently high fidelity. (ii) For the commitment phase, sampling a solitary arm significantly simplifies the analysis for canonical ETC, and its regret bound is guided by the probability of identifying a suboptimal arm at the end of exploration. On the contrary, we have the additional task of ensuring that our sampling fractions appropriately match the chosen discrete mixture at the end of exploration. (iii) For some arms, the exploration phase might already be longer than necessary. In this case, the worst-case sampling error scales as O ⁡ ( N ⁡ ( ε ) T ) O(\frac{N(\varepsilon)}{T}) . For the others, the PM-ETC-M commitment strategy ensures that we incur minimal regret. In Section I.3 , we show that on aggregate, the proposed sampling rule incurs a sublinear regret. Choosing ε \varepsilon . Theorem 4 is valid for any ε ∈ ℝ + \varepsilon\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+} , allowing freedom to appropriately choose the desired accuracy for the mixing coefficients. Observe that δ 01 ​ ( ε ) \delta_{01}(\varepsilon) is proportional to ε \varepsilon as shown in Lemma 8 , and N ⁡ ( ε ) N(\varepsilon) is inversely proportional to ε \varepsilon . Hence, while arbitrarily diminishing ε \varepsilon decreases the estimation regret, it may violate the condition that T &gt; N ⁡ ( ε ) T&gt;N(\varepsilon) . We chose ε \varepsilon to be small enough (small estimation regret), while conforming to the condition T &gt; N ⁡ ( ε ) T&gt;N(\varepsilon) in Theorem 4 (feasibility). The minimum feasible ε \varepsilon depends on q q and its connection to the sub-optimality gap as follows. Let us define β ≜ lim ε → 0 log ⁡ δ 12 ​ ( ε ) log ⁡ ε , \displaystyle\beta\triangleq\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{\log\delta_{12}(\varepsilon)}{\log\varepsilon}\ , (30) which quantifies how fast the minimum sub-optimality gap δ 12 ​ ( ε CLOSE \delta_{12}(\varepsilon ) diminishes as ε \varepsilon tends to 0 0 . For bandit models with discrete distributions supported on Ω \Omega , if | Ω | ≥ K |\Omega|\geq K , we have β ∈ { 1 , 2 } \beta\in\{1,2\} . Proof: See Appendix G . Based on the definition of β \beta , we next provide an ε \varepsilon -independent regret bound by setting the discretization interval as an appropriate function of q q and β \beta . (31) This choice of ε \varepsilon leads to the following regret bound. Proof: See Appendix I.5 . From Theorem 5 , we observe that the order of convergence in the horizon scales as O ⁡ ( T − γ 2 ​ β ​ q 2 ) O(T^{-\frac{\gamma}{2\beta}q^{2}}) . This regret term is evaluated and reported in Table 1 for various notions of PMs, which have their specific q q . Next, we present the regret guarantees for the two UCB-based algorithms, i.e., PM-UCB-M and CE-UCB-M. An important observation is that these algorithms yield weaker arm selection regret guarantees compared to PM-ETC-M. The regret degradations are the expense of not knowing the instance-dependent gaps. (34) The next theorem presents an ε \varepsilon -dependent regret for the PM-UCB-M and CE-UCB-M algorithms. Theorem 6 (PF/CE-UCB-M – ε \varepsilon -dependent) For any ε ∈ ℝ + \varepsilon\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+} , and distortion function h h with Hölder exponent q q , for all T &gt; max ⁡ { e K , T ⁡ ( ε ) } T&gt;\max\{{\rm e}^{K},T(\varepsilon)\} for π ​ { U , C } \pi\{{\rm U,C}\} we have ℜ 𝝂 π ​ ( T ) \displaystyle\mathfrak{R}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}^{\pi}(T) ≤ [ B h + 2 ​ ℒ ​ K ​ ( W q + 1 ) ] ​ [ 64 ε ​ ρ ​ T ​ ( 2 ​ e ​ log ⁡ T + 32 ) ] q + δ 01 ​ ( ε ) . \displaystyle\leq[B_{h}+2\mathcal{L}K(W^{q}+1)]\Bigg[\frac{64}{\sqrt{\varepsilon\rho T}}\Big(\sqrt{2{\rm e}\log T}+32\Big)\Bigg]^{q}+\delta_{01}(\varepsilon)\ . (35) Proof: See Appendix J.4 . Proof sketch. The distinction of the proof with that of the vanilla UCB, is that in the vanilla UCB, the general proof uses the fact that selecting any suboptimal arm more than O ⁡ ( log ⁡ T ) O(\log T) times is unlikely, and hence, the overall regret is bounded by O ⁡ ( 1 T ​ K ​ log ⁡ T ) O(\frac{1}{T}K\log T) . However, in our analyses, we have the new dimension of estimating the mixing coefficients and need these estimates to converge to an optimal choice. Such convergence requires that all arms be sampled at a rate linear in T T (unless an arm’s mixing coefficient is 0). Hence, selecting any arm O ⁡ ( log ⁡ T ) O(\log T) times is insufficient. The other key difference pertains to finding a bound on the mixing coefficient errors (estimation and convergence). Characterizing this bound hinges on two key steps: (i) the convergence of the UCB estimates ( 𝐚 t U \mathbf{a}_{t}^{\rm U} for PM-UCB-M and 𝐚 t C \mathbf{a}_{t}^{\rm C} for CE-UCB-M) to the discrete optimal solution 𝐚 ( 1 ) \mathbf{a}^{(1)} in probability, and (ii) a sublinear regret incurred in the process of tracking the mixing coefficient estimates using under-sampling. The first step is analyzed in Appendix J.3 , where we show that the probability of error for the PM-UCB-M and CE-UCB-M algorithms in identifying the discrete optimal mixture is upper bounded by T ⁡ ( ( 1 T 2 + 1 ) K − 1 ) T((\frac{1}{T^{2}}+1)^{K}-1) . The second step is analyzed in Lemma 21 in Appendix J.3 , in which we show that the regret incurred by the tracking block of the PM-UCB-M and CE-UCB-M algorithms is of the order O ⁡ ( K T ) O(\frac{K}{T}) . The regret upper bound is a combination of the regrets in these results. Choosing ε \varepsilon . Similarly to PM-ETC-M, we choose an ε \varepsilon that ensures T &gt; T ⁡ ( ε ) T&gt;T(\varepsilon) for the bound in Theorem 6 . We set ε = Θ ⁡ ( ( K 2 q ​ log ⁡ T T ) κ ) , where ​ κ ≜ ( 2 ​ β q + 2 ) − 1 . \displaystyle\varepsilon=\Theta\left(\left(K^{\frac{2}{q}}\;\frac{\log T}{T}\right)^{\kappa}\right)\ ,\quad\mbox{where}\;\;\kappa\triangleq\Big(\frac{2\beta}{q}+2\Big)^{-1}\ . (36) Based on these choices, we specify the ε \varepsilon -independent regret in the next theorem. We note that, similar to the ETC-based algorithm, characterizing β \beta might not be feasible. Theorem 7 (PF/CE-UCB-M – ε \varepsilon -independent) Under the conditions of Theorem 6 , we have the following regret guarantees. When β \beta exists, the regret upper bound for π ∈ { U , C } \pi\in\{{\rm U,C}\} is ℜ 𝝂 π ​ ( T ) ≤ O ⁡ ( K c U ​ [ log ⁡ T T ] q ​ κ ) , where c U ≜ max ⁡ { q ⁡ ( 1 + 2 ​ κ q ) , 1 − κ } . \displaystyle\mathfrak{R}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}^{\pi}(T)\leq O\left(K^{c_{\rm U}}\Big[\frac{\log T}{T}\Big]^{q\kappa}\right)\ ,\qquad\mbox{where}\quad c_{\rm U}\triangleq\max\left\{q\left(1+\frac{2\kappa}{q}\right),{1-\kappa}\right\}. (37) Proof: See Appendix J.5 . An important observation is the following contrast between the regrets characterized for the mixture algorithms and their canonical ETC and UCB counterparts: the canonical ETC and UCB algorithms generally exhibit the same regret, even though ETC requires access to instance-dependent parameters. In the mixtures setting, however, access to instance-dependent parameters yields better arm selection regret guarantees for the ETC-type algorithm (i.e., PM-ETC-M). After setting an appropriate discretization level ε \varepsilon , the relationship becomes significantly more nuanced, and the performance of PM-ETC-M and PM-UCB-M depends on the choice of the utility. Note that when we set ε \varepsilon , we assume to know β \beta – and hence, PM-ETC-M and PM-UCB-M have the same amount of information in this setting. Regret Bounds for Important Cases. We specialize our general results to several widely-used PMs with differentiable distortion functions and report their regret bounds in Table 1 . For the PMs listed in this table β \beta takes values in { 1 , 2 } \{1,2\} depending on whether the underlying distribution models. Except for PHT measure and Wang’s Right-tail deviation, where we restrict ourselves to bounded distributions, these underlying distributions include continuous sub-Gaussian distributions and discrete distributions with | Ω | ≥ K |\Omega|\geq K . While reporting the regret bounds in Table 1 , we used the Hölder continuity exponents specified in Table 4 (Appendix F ). We remark that the ε \varepsilon -dependent regret bounds depend only on the Hölder continuity constant q q (Theorems 4 and 6 ). On the other hand, the ε \varepsilon -independent regret bounds will additionally depend on q q and β \beta (Theorems 5 and 7 ). From Table 1 , we observe that no algorithm uniformly dominates the other . Under the assumption of solitary arm, i.e., when the optimal solution lies on the boundaries where we have β = 1 \beta=1 , PM-ETC-M performs better than PM-UCB-M for several measures, including mean and Gini deviation, as in Table 1 . However, when the optimal solution does not lie on the boundaries of the Δ K − 1 \Delta^{K-1} , where we have β = 2 \beta=2 , PM - UCB - M actually outperforms PM - ETC - M on measures such as PHT (see Table 1 ). Intuitively, PM - ETC - M commits in advance to a single mixture coefficient using the minimum sub-optimality gap, whereas in PM - UCB - M analysis, this gap is only used to choose the discretization level ε \varepsilon that conforms to T ⁡ ( ε ) T(\varepsilon) . We do something smaller while choosing a feasible ε \varepsilon for PM-ETC-M. As a result, PM - UCB - M’s less reliance on gap information results in worse regret bounds. Finally, we comment on Gini deviation, which served as a motivation example in Section 1 . In the case of Gini deviation, we observe that for arm selection regret, the PM-ETC-M algorithm achieves O ⁡ ( K T ) O(\frac{K}{T}) regret while PM-UCB-M achieves O ⁡ ( K T ) O(\frac{K}{\sqrt{T}}) regret, ignoring polylogarithmic factors. However, when we optimize the algorithms for the discretization level ε \varepsilon , we observe an order-wise improvement in the regret of the algorithms. For a all continuous and majority of discrete distributions, β \beta is guaranteed to exist and is characterized by Lemma 4 . Nevertheless, for discrete models, when the cardinality of the sample space is less than K K , i.e., | Ω | &lt; K |\Omega|&lt;K , β \beta will be degenerate for the choice of the discretization strategy we have. This is because the minimum sub-optimality gap δ 12 ​ ( ε ) \delta_{12}(\varepsilon) can be arbitrarily small for certain PMs and bandit models. For instance, in the K K -arm Bernoulli bandit where some arms have mean values strictly larger than 1 2 \frac{1}{2} and the others strictly smaller than 1 2 \frac{1}{2} , under Gini deviation and the uniform discretization approach we have adopted, the minimum gap δ 12 ​ ( ε ) \delta_{12}(\varepsilon) can shrink arbitrarily fast, rendering its rate of decay with shrinking ε \varepsilon undefined. One solution to circumvent such cases, is modifying the PM-ETC-M algorithm by replacing δ 12 \delta_{12} with δ 13 \delta_{13} defined as 𝐚 ( 3 ) ≜ arg ​ max 𝐚 ∈ Δ ε K − 1 : 𝐚 ( 3 ) ≠ 𝐚 ( 1 ) , 𝐚 ( 2 ) V ( 𝐚 , 𝔽 ) , and δ 13 ( ε ) \displaystyle\mathbf{a}^{(3)}\triangleq\argmax\limits_{\mathbf{a}\in\Delta^{K-1}_{\varepsilon}:\mathbf{a}^{(3)}\neq\mathbf{a}^{(1)},\mathbf{a}^{(2)}}\;V(\mathbf{a},\mathbb{F})\ ,\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\delta_{13}(\varepsilon) ≜ V ⁡ ( 𝐚 ( 1 ) , 𝔽 ) − V ⁡ ( 𝐚 ( 3 ) , 𝔽 ) . \displaystyle\triangleq V\Big(\mathbf{a}^{(1)},\mathbb{F}\Big)-V\Big(\mathbf{a}^{(3)},\mathbb{F}\Big)\ . (41) This choice of ε \varepsilon leads to the following ε \varepsilon -independent regret bound under the existence of β ¯ {\bar{\beta}} . A K K -arm Bernoulli bandit where K &gt; 2 K&gt;2 constitutes a corner case in which β \beta does not exist, as the conditions of Lemma 4 are violated. Consequently, we analytically derive the β ¯ {\bar{\beta}} values for K K -arm Bernoulli bandit models and report their values in Table 3 . In Table 3 , for the first 5 5 rows, both β \beta and β ¯ \bar{\beta} was identified. However, for the last 4 4 rows, when the distortion function is non-monotone, β \beta could not be identified. For some PMs, we have a range of β \beta values. The corresponding regret bounds for the PM-ETC-M algorithm is provided in Table 2 , as obtained from Theorem 8 . The first column of regret is when the suboptimality gap considered is δ 13 ​ ( ε ) \delta_{13}(\varepsilon) , while the second column of regret is when the suboptimality gap considered is δ 12 ​ ( ε ) \delta_{12}(\varepsilon) . For PMs associated with monotonically increasing distortion functions h h , Lemma 16 establishes that such PMs are maximized by solitary arms. In these cases, an alternative discretization strategy for the PM-ETC-M algorithm, distinct from ( 15 ), can be employed by setting ε = 1 \varepsilon=1 . This choice implies that the estimation regret δ 01 = 0 \delta_{01}=0 , and only the arm selection regret remains, as reported in the last column of Table 2 . While under the sub-optimality gap δ 12 ​ ( ε ) \delta_{12}(\varepsilon) , we report better regret guarantees, for the sub-optimality gap δ 13 ​ ( ε ) \delta_{13}(\varepsilon) , we can characterize all β ¯ \bar{\beta} values for the PMs in Table 3 . For some PMs, we obtain exact values of β \beta and β ¯ {\bar{\beta}} , and for other PMs, we determine valid ranges. These values are characterized in Appendix H . Analyzing these values involves two key steps. First, we characterize an upper-bound on β ¯ \bar{\beta} by bounding δ 13 ​ ( ε ) \delta_{13}(\varepsilon) from below. Subsequently, we establish a lower-bound on β ¯ \bar{\beta} by bounding δ 13 ​ ( ε ) \delta_{13}(\varepsilon) from above. If the two bounds match, we have obtained an exact value of β ¯ \bar{\beta} . Alternatively, we report a range for it. In Table 3 , we report β ¯ {\bar{\beta}} for several PMs, where for some of the PMs, β \beta does not exist.
