Hence, for a discretization interval length ε \varepsilon , we face an O ⁡ ( 1 ε K ) O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{K}}) -dimensional discrete optimization problem for both the PM-ETC-M and the PM-UCB-M algorithms. Additionally, these algorithms crucially depend on the horizon information T T . To circumvent these shortcomings, in this section, we propose the C onfidence-guided I nterval R efinement and T racking (CIRT) algorithm, denoted by π = IR \pi={\rm IR} . This algorithm has two key features. (i) It resolves the computational bottleneck of the PM-ETC-M and PM-UCB-M algorithms. (ii) It is an anytime algorithm , i.e., it does not require the knowledge of the horizon T T . Designing such an algorithm introduces the following two challenges. • Challenges in exploration. Knowing the horizon helps the learner to calibrate the rate of exploration explicitly in terms of the horizon. For instance, the horizon-dependent UCB-based algorithms perform an initial horizon-dependent explicit exploration that ensures sufficiently accurate CDF estimates before starting the UCB-based sampling. This is no longer feasible for the anytime algorithm, which requires designing a horizon-adaptive explicit exploration routine to ensure high-fidelity CDF estimates. In CIRT, we take inspiration from the best arm identification literature [ 28 , 27 ] to devise an adaptive exploration scheme, which at round t t , ensures that each arm is explored as much as an appropriately designed increasing function of t t . • Constructing confidence sets. Distribution confidence sequences used in the horizon-dependent algorithms are carefully constructed so that the probability of error events decreases as a function of the horizon. As a result, these confidence sequences explicitly depend on the known horizon. In contrast, when the horizon is unknown, confidence sequences can only satisfy a fixed, horizon-independent guarantee on error probabilities, leading to weaker performance guarantees compared to their horizon-dependent counterparts. To address these challenges as well as the exponential growth of the discrete space’s cardinality, the central idea of CIRT is to start with a coarse discretization scheme and, over time, adaptively refine it based on the collected data up to a pre-specified granularity. This facilitates solving a low-dimensional optimization (cardinality independent of K K ) at each step. The algorithm proceeds in two steps. The first step, which we call the interval refinement step, focuses on eliminating intervals from the simplex that are likely sub-optimal and splitting the retained intervals into finer discretization lengths. The second step, referred to as the tracking step, focuses on minimizing the average regret while committing the arm selection to match an estimated discrete optimal coefficient from the previous step. 5.1 Algorithm Description 5.1.1 Interval Refinement Process. In the first step, the CIRT algorithm adopts a confidence-guided methodology to adaptively eliminate intervals from the simplex that are likely sub-optimal and split the retained intervals into finer discretization lengths. This step is broken down into multiple phases indexed by ℓ ∈ [ L ] \ell\in[L] , such that at the end of the last phase L L , the discretization length adheres to a pre-specified accuracy ϵ \epsilon . Here, the algorithm takes ϵ \epsilon as an input, which is the minimum length of the discretization interval prescribed by the user. Furthermore, CIRT takes an input A A , which specifies the order of the optimization problem that the learner can afford at each step. Specifically, A A denotes the number of discrete mixing coefficients along each coordinate for performing any optimization at each instant. In each phase, the algorithm shrinks the discretization interval by an order 1 / A 1/A . Hence, the number of phases L L for conforming to the accuracy ϵ \epsilon can be readily verified to be L = ⌈ log A 2 ⁡ ( 1 / ϵ ) ⌉ L=\lceil\log_{\frac{A}{2}}(1/\epsilon)\rceil . Each phase ℓ ∈ [ L ] \ell\in[L] is characterized by three decision rules. 1. A sampling rule , which specifies the arm to be sampled at the next instant within each phase. 2. A stopping time , which governs the length of the phase. 3. An interval retention decision , which specifies the intervals in the simplex Δ K − 1 \Delta^{K-1} that are retained for the next phase. For specifying these rules, the CIRT algorithm creates a sequence of “refined” discrete sets { Δ ℓ K − 1 : ℓ ∈ [ L ] } \{\Delta_{\ell}^{K-1}:\ell\in[L]\} . For the first phase, we initialize Δ 1 K − 1 = Δ 1 / A K − 1 \Delta_{1}^{K-1}=\Delta_{1/A}^{K-1} , where Δ ε K − 1 \Delta_{\varepsilon}^{K-1} has been specified in ( 15 ) for any ε ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \varepsilon\in(0,1) . Subsequently, CIRT gradually refines the set of mixtures over the subsequent phases, and the exact refinement procedure, i.e., Δ ℓ K − 1 \Delta_{\ell}^{K-1} for each phase ℓ &gt; 1 \ell&gt;1 will be formally specified shortly (see ( 5.1.1 )). Furthermore, based on the sets Δ ℓ K − 1 \Delta_{\ell}^{K-1} in phase ℓ ∈ [ L ] \ell\in[L] , we denote the best mixing coefficient computed according to the estimated CDFs up to the current time instant t ∈ ℕ t\in{\mathbb{N}} by 𝐚 t IR ≜ arg ​ max 𝐚 ∈ Δ ℓ K − 1 ⁡ U h ​ ( ∑ i ∈ [ K ] a ⁡ ( i ) ​ 𝔽 i , t IR ) . \displaystyle\mathbf{a}_{t}^{\rm IR}\;\triangleq\;\argmax_{\mathbf{a}\in\Delta^{K-1}_{\ell}}U_{h}\left(\sum_{i\in[K]}a(i)\mathbb{F}_{i,t}^{\rm IR}\right)\ . (43) Stopping Time. We begin by characterizing the stopping mechanism of the CIRT algorithm, which specifies the length of each phase. At any phase ℓ ∈ [ L ] \ell\in[L] , CIRT decides to end the current phase when it is confident about the sub-optimal intervals in the phase. We design the stopping rule such that at the end of each phase, the correct interval, i.e, the one containing the optimal solution, will be chosen with a high probability. We denote this probability by δ \delta , which is provided as an input to the algorithm. Accordingly, the distribution confidence sets defined for the CIRT algorithm are given by 𝒞 t IR ( i ) ≜ { η ∈ ℳ i \displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{t}^{\rm IR}(i)\triangleq\bigg\{\eta\in\mathcal{M}_{i} : ‖ 𝔽 i , t IR − η ‖ W ≤ 16 e ​ log ⁡ 2 / δ K + 32 τ t IR ​ ( i ) } , \displaystyle:\left\lVert\mathbb{F}_{i,t}^{\rm IR}-\eta\right\rVert_{\rm W}\leq 16\ \frac{\sqrt{{\rm e}\log{2/\delta_{K}}}+32}{\sqrt{\tau^{\rm IR}_{t}(i)}}\bigg\}\ , (44) where we have defined δ K ≜ ( ( 1 + δ ) 1 / K − 1 ) . \displaystyle\delta_{K}\triangleq\left(\left(1+\delta\right)^{1/K}-1\right)\ . (45) Furthermore, similar to PM-UCB-M, for any discrete mixing coefficient 𝐚 ∈ Δ ℓ K − 1 \mathbf{a}\in\Delta^{K-1}_{\ell} at a given phase ℓ ∈ [ L ] \ell\in[L] and at any time instant t ∈ ℕ t\in{\mathbb{N}} , we define upper and lower confidence bounds for the estimated utilities as UCB t IR ​ ( 𝐚 ) \displaystyle{\rm UCB}_{t}^{\rm IR}(\mathbf{a})\; ≜ max η i ∈ C t ​ ( i ) , ∀ i ∈ [ K ] ⁡ U h ​ ( ∑ i ∈ [ K ] a ⁡ ( i ) ​ η i ) , \displaystyle\triangleq\;\max_{\eta_{i}\in C_{t}(i),\forall i\in[K]}U_{h}\left(\sum\limits_{i\in[K]}a(i)\eta_{i}\right), (46) and LCB t IR ​ ( 𝐚 ) \displaystyle\text{and}\quad{\rm LCB}_{t}^{\rm IR}(\mathbf{a})\; ≜ min η i ∈ C t ​ ( i ) , ∀ i ∈ [ K ] ⁡ U h ​ ( ∑ i ∈ [ K ] a ⁡ ( i ) ​ η i ) . \displaystyle\triangleq\;\min_{\eta_{i}\in C_{t}(i),\forall i\in[K]}U_{h}\left(\sum\limits_{i\in[K]}a(i)\eta_{i}\right)\ . (47) Using these confidence bounds, we define the stopping rule as the earliest time instant at which the lower confidence bound of the chosen mixing coefficient stops overlapping with the upper confidence bound of all other mixing coefficients. After this point, the learner pauses the sampling process and discretizes the space again for the next phase according to ( 5.1.1 ). Formally, for phase ℓ \ell we define the stopping time as τ ( ℓ ) ≜ inf { t ∈ ℕ : LCB t IR ( 𝐚 t ) &gt; max 𝐚 ∈ Δ ℓ K − 1 : 𝐚 ≠ 𝐚 t IR UCB t IR ( 𝐚 ) } . \displaystyle\tau^{(\ell)}\;\triangleq\;\inf\Big\{t\in{\mathbb{N}}:{\rm LCB}_{t}^{\rm IR}\left(\mathbf{a}_{t}\right)&gt;\max_{\mathbf{a}\in\Delta^{K-1}_{\ell}:\mathbf{a}\neq\mathbf{a}_{t}^{\rm IR}}{\rm UCB}_{t}^{\rm IR}(\mathbf{a})\Big\}\ . (48) Interval Retention Decision. For any phase ℓ ∈ [ L ] \ell\in[L] , at the stopping time τ ( ℓ ) \tau^{(\ell)} , CIRT forms an interval retention decision for performing a more fine-grained optimization in the next phase. To specify the retained interval, the algorithm first obtains the best discrete mixing coefficient at stopping, i.e., 𝐛 ( ℓ ) ≜ 𝐚 τ ℓ IR . \displaystyle\mathbf{b}^{(\ell)}\;\triangleq\;\mathbf{a}_{\tau_{\ell}}^{\rm IR}\ . (49) Based on this, the retained interval along each coordinate i ∈ [ K ] i\in[K] is chosen as the interval of length ( 2 A ) ℓ \left(\frac{2}{A}\right)^{\ell} , which contains b ( ℓ ) ​ ( i ) b^{(\ell)}(i) as the midpoint. More specifically, we zoom into the interval around the empirical best discrete coefficient from the previous phase, i.e., the interval [ b ( ℓ ) ​ ( i ) − 2 ℓ − 1 A ℓ , b ( ℓ ) ​ ( i ) + 2 ℓ − 1 A ℓ ] , \displaystyle\left[b^{(\ell)}(i)-\frac{2^{\ell-1}}{A^{\ell}},b^{(\ell)}(i)+\frac{2^{\ell-1}}{A^{\ell}}\right]\ , (50) along each coordinate i ∈ [ K ] i\in[K] . These intervals are further discretized into A A levels, and the set of discrete mixing coefficients is constructed such that these coefficients conform to the probability constraint. More formally, we define Δ ℓ + 1 K − 1 ≜ { \displaystyle\Delta^{K-1}_{\ell+1}\triangleq\bigg\{ 𝐚 : n ∈ { ℕ ∪ 0 } , a ( i ) ≜ 2 ℓ ​ n A ℓ + 1 , a ( i ) ∈ [ b ( ℓ ) ( i ) − 2 ℓ − 1 A ℓ , b ( ℓ ) ( i ) + 2 ℓ − 1 A ℓ ] , 1 T 𝐚 = 1 } . \displaystyle\mathbf{a}:\;\;n\in\{{\mathbb{N}}\cup 0\},\;a(i)\triangleq\frac{2^{\ell}n}{A^{\ell+1}},\;a(i)\in\left[b^{(\ell)}(i)-\frac{2^{\ell-1}}{A^{\ell}},b^{(\ell)}(i)+\frac{2^{\ell-1}}{A^{\ell}}\right]\;,\;\mathbf{1}^{T}\mathbf{a}=1\bigg\}\ . Sampling Rule. Having characterized the stopping and the interval shrinking mechanisms, we will next specify the sampling process that the algorithm adopts within each phase. The sampling mechanism resembles that of the PM-UCB-M algorithm, with the following two exceptions: 1. It uses sublinear explicit exploration scheme as opposed to a linear order of exploration of the PM-UCB-M algorithm. 2. At each step, it chooses the discrete solution that maximizes the empirical utility instead of UCB and this maximizing step is performed on the lower dimensional simplex Δ ℓ K − 1 \Delta_{\ell}^{K-1} as opposed to Δ ε K − 1 \Delta^{K-1}_{\varepsilon} , which was the a key element of the PM-UCB-M algorithm. Explicit Exploration: The PM-UCB-M algorithm, which presumes the knowledge of the horizon T T , samples each arm at least 1 4 ​ ρ ​ T ​ ε \frac{1}{4}\rho T\varepsilon number of times which ensures that no arm is over-explored due to the explicit exploration phase. The UCB sampling rule, together with under-sampling, carefully balances the sampling process such that the arm selection fractions may converge to the (discrete) optimal coefficients, having high-fidelity estimates of the arm CDFs as a result of the initial horizon-dependent explicit exploration phase. However, in the premise of an anytime algorithm, the horizon is unknown . Consequently, the linear exploration scheme, which is suited for PM-UCB-M, does not work for CIRT. Hence, rather than resorting to an exploration phase followed by the UCB and under-sampling steps, the CIRT algorithm, at every instant t ∈ ℕ t\in{\mathbb{N}} , has to decide whether any arm is under-explored, or the learner should resort to UCB-based under-sampling. Striking a balance between explicit exploration and under-sampling may not be achieved if the order of explicit exploration is linear , since some arms may be heavily over-sampled. Instead, we resort to a sub-linear exploration for each arm, and let the under-sampling procedure adjust the allocation fractions for the arms which require a linear order of exploration. Such a sub-linear exploration scheme has been investigated in the best arm identification literature, see, e.g., [ 28 , 27 ] . CIRT requires an exploration exponent ξ &gt; 0 \xi&gt;0 which specifies the order of explicit exploration of the arms for accurate estimation. Accordingly, we define the set of under-explored arms E t ≜ { i ∈ [ K ] : τ t IR ​ ( i ) ≤ ⌈ ( t K ) 1 1 + ξ ⌉ } , \displaystyle E_{t}\triangleq\left\{i\in[K]:\tau_{t}^{\rm IR}(i)\leq\left\lceil\bigg(\frac{t}{K}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{1+\xi}}\right\rceil\right\}\ , (51) as the set of arms which have been sampled fewer than ⌈ ( t K ) 1 1 + ξ ⌉ \lceil(\frac{t}{K})^{\frac{1}{1+\xi}}\rceil times up to t ∈ ℕ t\in{\mathbb{N}} , where ξ \xi dictates the order of the sub-linear exploration. Arm selection: At each instant t ∈ ℕ t\in{\mathbb{N}} , CIRT samples an under-explored arm i ∈ E t i\in E_{t} , if the set E t ≠ ∅ E_{t}\neq\emptyset . Otherwise, if E t = ∅ E_{t}=\emptyset , CIRT samples an arm guided by the current UCB-maximizing coefficient, following the under-sampling principle. The CIRT arm selection rule is specified by A t + 1 ≜ { arg ​ min i ∈ E t τ t IR ( i ) , if ​ E t ≠ ∅ arg ​ max i ∈ [ K ] ⁡ { ta t − τ t IR ​ ( i ) } , if ​ E t = ∅ . \displaystyle A_{t+1}\triangleq\begin{cases}\argmin_{i\in E_{t}}\tau_{t}^{\rm IR}(i),\qquad\qquad\qquad&amp;\text{if}\;E_{t}\neq\emptyset\\ &amp;\\ \argmax_{i\in[K]}\{ta_{t}-\tau^{\rm IR}_{t}(i)\},\;&amp;\text{if}\;E_{t}=\emptyset\end{cases}\ . (52) 5.1.2 Tracking Process The interval refinement distills the set of discrete mixing coefficients such that the optimal discrete coefficient is contained at the end of L L phases with a high probability. We use this property to commit to a mixing coefficient, up to a permissible estimation regret. Specifically, at the end of the interval refinement step, we perform an additional round of interval refinement by zooming around a small vicinity of the last chosen mixing coefficient 𝐚 ( L ) \mathbf{a}^{(L)} , and obtain Δ ϵ K − 1 \Delta_{\epsilon}^{K-1} according to the following rule.( 53 ). Δ ϵ K − 1 ≜ { \displaystyle\Delta^{K-1}_{\epsilon}\triangleq\bigg\{ 𝐚 : n ∈ ℕ , a ( i ) ≜ ( n + 1 2 ) 2 L A L + 1 , a ( i ) ∈ [ b ( L ) ( i ) − 2 L − 1 A L , b ( L ) ( i ) + 2 L − 1 A L ] , 1 T 𝐚 = 1 } . \displaystyle\mathbf{a}:\;\;n\in{\mathbb{N}},\;a(i)\triangleq\left(n+\frac{1}{2}\right)\frac{2^{L}}{A^{L+1}},\;a(i)\in\left[b^{(L)}(i)-\frac{2^{L-1}}{A^{L}},b^{(L)}(i)+\frac{2^{L-1}}{A^{L}}\right]\;,\;\mathbf{1}^{T}\mathbf{a}=1\bigg\}\ . (53) Subsequently, we select the discrete mixing coefficient 𝐛 ( L + 1 ) \mathbf{b}^{(L+1)} that maximizes the empirical utility such that 𝐛 ( L + 1 ) = arg ​ max 𝐚 ∈ Δ ϵ K − 1 ⁡ U h ​ ( ∑ i ∈ [ K ] a ⁡ ( i ) ​ 𝔽 i ) , \displaystyle\mathbf{b}^{(L+1)}=\argmax_{\mathbf{a}\in\Delta_{\epsilon}^{K-1}}U_{h}\left(\sum_{i\in[K]}a(i)\mathbb{F}_{i}\right)\ , (54) and perform under-sampling according to the chosen mixture similarly to ( 52 ). Formally, for any t &gt; τ ( L ) t&gt;\tau^{(L)} , we have A t + 1 ≜ { arg ​ min i ∈ E t τ t IR ( i ) , if ​ E t ≠ ∅ arg ​ max i ∈ [ K ] ⁡ { tb ( L + 1 ) − τ t IR ​ ( i ) } , if ​ E t = ∅ . \displaystyle A_{t+1}\triangleq\begin{cases}\argmin_{i\in E_{t}}\tau_{t}^{\rm IR}(i),\qquad\qquad\qquad&amp;\text{if}\;E_{t}\neq\emptyset\\ &amp;\\ \argmax_{i\in[K]}\{tb^{(L+1)}-\tau^{\rm IR}_{t}(i)\},\;&amp;\text{if}\;E_{t}=\emptyset\end{cases}\ . As discussed in Section 5.1 , one of the key features of the CIRT algorithm is its computational efficiency. Specifically, CIRT is adaptive to the learner’s computational budget A A . In order for CIRT to exhibit sublinear regret guarantees, it is imperative that an optimal mixing coefficient is retained at the end of every phase, or else, the tracking procedure fails to perform arm selections that mimic an optimal mixing coefficient. Furthermore, since the phases in the interval refinement step are adaptive to the data, we must certify that the stopping time in each phase is finite on average. These properties would collectively ensure a sublinear regret for the CIRT algorithm. We begin by establishing an upper bound on the misidentification error, i.e., the probability that an optimal solution is not contained in the retained interval at the end of the interval retention phase. Subsequently, we will analyze the average interval length of any phase ℓ ∈ [ L ] \ell\in[L] of the interval refinement step and show that it is guaranteed to be finite on average. Finally, leveraging the error guarantee and finiteness of the interval lengths, we will provide an upper bound on the regret due to the CIRT sampling rule. Probability of Error. We begin by providing an upper bound on the probability that at the end of the interval retention phase, CIRT zooms into an interval that does not contain an optimal mixing coefficient. We first provide a few notations. Let us define Σ ℓ + 1 K − 1 \Sigma^{K-1}_{\ell+1} as the continuous counterpart of the discrete simplex Δ ℓ + 1 K − 1 \Delta^{K-1}_{\ell+1} in any phase ℓ ∈ [ L ] \ell\in[L] , as Σ ℓ + 1 K − 1 ≜ { 𝜶 ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] K : α ( i ) ∈ [ b ( ℓ ) ( i ) − 2 ℓ − 1 A ℓ , b ( ℓ ) ( i ) + 2 ℓ − 1 A ℓ ] , 1 T 𝜶 = 1 } . \displaystyle\Sigma^{K-1}_{\ell+1}\triangleq\bigg\{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in[0,1]^{K}:\alpha(i)\in\left[b^{(\ell)}(i)-\frac{2^{\ell-1}}{A^{\ell}},b^{(\ell)}(i)+\frac{2^{\ell-1}}{A^{\ell}}\right]\;,\;\mathbf{1}^{T}\boldsymbol{\alpha}=1\bigg\}\ . Furthermore, since L L depends on the prescribed accuracy ϵ \epsilon , let us denote Σ ϵ K − 1 ≜ Σ L + 1 K − 1 \Sigma^{K-1}_{\epsilon}\triangleq\Sigma^{K-1}_{L+1} . Lemma 5 Consider a K K -arm Bernoulli bandit instance. For any PM, using any sampling rule paired with the stopping rule defined in ( 48 ) and the interval retention decision defined in ( 5.1.1 ), the probability of retaining an optimal mixing coefficient at the end of the interval retention phase satisfies ℙ 𝝂 π ​ ( 𝜶 ⋆ ∈ Σ ϵ K − 1 ) ≥ 1 − L ​ δ , \displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}^{\pi}\Big(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\star}\in\Sigma^{K-1}_{\epsilon}\Big)\geq 1-L\delta\ , (56) where we recall that L = ⌈ log A 2 ⁡ ( 1 / ϵ ) ⌉ L=\left\lceil\log_{\frac{A}{2}}(1/\epsilon)\right\rceil . Proof: See Appendix L.1 . Proof sketch. The proof follows from upper-bounding the probability that an optimal mixing coefficient is not contained in the refined continuous set Σ ℓ K − 1 \Sigma_{\ell}^{K-1} at any phase ℓ ∈ [ L ] \ell\in[L] , followed by a subsequent union bound over the phases. To obtain the phase-specific error bound, we leverage the fact that with probability at least δ \delta , the true arm CDFs are contained in the respective distribution confidence sets (i.e., the confidence sets are sufficiently accurate). As a result, when the lower confidence bound of the current empirical best mixing coefficient exceeds the upper confidence bound for all other candidate coefficients, which is the premise of the stopping rule in ( 48 ), with high probability, we have identified the discrete optimizer of the current phase. Finiteness of the Phase Durations. Next, we show that the length of any phase ℓ ∈ [ L ] \ell\in[L] is finite on average. A crucial intermediate step to this result is to show that the explicit exploration scheme, as defined in ( 52 ), ensures that each arm is explored at a sublinear rate almost surely. The immediate consequence of this step is a guarantee on the estimation accuracy of the arm CDFs. Alternatively, if arms are under-explored, it might result in a poor estimation accuracy, resulting in sub-optimal choices of interval retention. The next result, which has been significantly leveraged in the best arm identification literature [ 28 , 29 , 27 ] , establishes an almost sure lower bound on the frequency of arm selection. Lemma 6 Due to explicit exploration of CIRT’s sampling rule in ( 52 ), at every instant t ∈ ℕ t\in{\mathbb{N}} , we almost surely have τ t ​ ( i ) ≥ ( t K ) 1 1 + γ − 1 . \displaystyle\tau_{t}(i)\geq\left(\frac{t}{K}\right)^{\frac{1}{1+\gamma}}-1\ . (57) Proof: The proof follows the same line of arguments as [ 28 , Lemma 17] . Next, we show that for any phase ℓ ∈ [ L ] \ell\in[L] , its length is finite on average. Note that the following result may be viewed as a sample complexity guarantee for estimation. Specifically, the lemma establishes that in order to achieve an estimation accuracy no smaller than ϵ \epsilon , the phased estimation routine of the CIRT algorithm uses a finite number of samples. Hence, for a sufficiently large horizon, the regret of the CIRT sampling routine is purely guided by the tracking procedure. While it is sufficient to show the finiteness of the phase lengths for the purpose of this paper, investigating an instance-dependent upper bound on the sample complexity is a promising direction for future research. Lemma 7 For each phase ℓ ∈ [ L ] \ell\in[L] , the CIRT algorithm comprising the sampling rule in ( 52 ) and the stopping rule in ( 48 ) satisfies 𝔼 𝝂 IR ​ [ τ ( ℓ ) ] &lt; + ∞ . \displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}^{\rm IR}\Big[\tau^{(\ell)}\Big]&lt;+\infty\ . (58) Proof: See Appendix L.2 . Proof sketch. The core idea in establishing a finite phase length on average is the observation that the distribution confidence sets defined in ( 44 ) are monotonically decreasing functions of time, which follows from Lemma 6 . Consequently, there exists a finite instant at which point these sets have shrunk enough to become smaller than the sub-optimality gap in utilities between the discrete optimal coefficient and any other mixing coefficient. Leveraging the high-probability event that the true distributions are contained in the distribution confidence sets, the claim follows by noticing that the CIRT algorithm stops the current phase when the confidence sets between the empirical best coefficient cease to overlap with all other coefficients. Specifically, when the diameter of distribution confidence sets falls below the sub-optimality gap, the CIRT algorithm stops the current phase since any overlap between the distribution confidence sets is no longer feasible. We show that the time instant at which this happens is finite on average. 5.3 Regret Analysis In this section, we provide an upper bound on the regret incurred by the CIRT algorithm. Unlike Section 4 , which quantifies the average regrets of the PM-ETC-M and the PM-UCB-M algorithms, we report a probabilistic bound on the regret of the CIRT algorithm. Specifically, with slight abuse of notation, we redefine the regret for the CIRT algorithm as ℜ 𝝂 IR ​ ( T ) ≜ U h ​ ( ∑ i ∈ [ K ] α ⋆ ​ ( i ) ​ 𝔽 i ) − U h ​ ( ∑ i ∈ [ K ] τ T IR ​ ( i ) T ​ 𝔽 i ) \displaystyle\mathfrak{R}^{\rm IR}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}(T)\triangleq U_{h}\left(\sum_{i\in[K]}\alpha^{\star}(i)\mathbb{F}_{i}\right)-U_{h}\left(\sum_{i\in[K]}\frac{\tau_{T}^{\rm IR}(i)}{T}\mathbb{F}_{i}\right) (59) Then, we can decompose the regret into estimation regret and sampling error . ℜ 𝝂 IR ​ ( T ) \displaystyle\mathfrak{R}^{\rm IR}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}(T) = U h ​ ( ∑ i ∈ [ K ] α ⋆ ​ ( i ) ​ 𝔽 i ) − U h ​ ( ∑ i ∈ [ K ] a T IR ​ ( i ) ​ 𝔽 i ) ⏟ ≜ δ τ ​ ( T ) ​ (estimation regret) + U h ​ ( ∑ i ∈ [ K ] a T IR ​ ( i ) ​ 𝔽 i ) − U h ​ ( ∑ i ∈ [ K ] τ T IR ​ ( i ) T ​ 𝔽 i ) ⏟ ≜ H 2 ​ ( T ) ​ (sampling error) . \displaystyle=\underbrace{U_{h}\left(\sum_{i\in[K]}\alpha^{\star}(i)\mathbb{F}_{i}\right)-U_{h}\left(\sum_{i\in[K]}a^{\rm IR}_{T}(i)\mathbb{F}_{i}\right)}_{\triangleq\;\delta_{\tau}(T)\text{ (estimation regret)}}+\underbrace{U_{h}\left(\sum_{i\in[K]}a^{\rm IR}_{T}(i)\mathbb{F}_{i}\right)-U_{h}\left(\sum_{i\in[K]}\frac{\tau_{T}^{\rm IR}(i)}{T}\mathbb{F}_{i}\right)}_{\triangleq\;H_{2}(T)\text{ (sampling error)}}\ . (60) The only difference between the regrets defined in ( 8 ) and ( 59 ) is the absence of the expectation operator with respect to the policy. The regret defined in ( 59 ) is stochastic, and in Theorem 9 , we provide a high probability upper bound on it due to the CIRT algorithm. Before stating the theorem, we remark that the result holds only for Bernoulli bandits. This is an inherent consequence of adaptive discretization, and the fact the probability of error bound in Lemma 5 has been shown to hold for Bernoulli bandits. While the result may seem restrictive, we would like to remark that even a refinement oracle , that knows the underlying arms’ distributions, and forms interval refinement decisions based on this information, is not guaranteed to recover the best discrete mixing coefficient at the end of the refinement phase. Such a refinement oracle has been shown to retain the optimal mixing coefficient for Bernoulli bandits in Lemma 24 . While extending this result to other distributions is interesting, we conjecture that it may not hold for distributions that yield non-concave PMs. Next, we state an upper bound on the regret of the CIRT algorithm for Bernoulli bandits. Theorem 9 Let 𝛎 \boldsymbol{\nu} denote a K K -arm Bernoulli bandit instance. For any ϵ ∈ ℝ + \epsilon\in{\mathbb{R}}^{+} and distortion function h h with Hölder exponent q q , there exist a stochastic time τ ϵ \tau^{\epsilon} such that with a probability at least 1 − δ ⁡ ⌈ log A 2 ⁡ ( 1 / ϵ ) ⌉ 1-\delta\lceil\log_{\frac{A}{2}}(1/\epsilon)\rceil , we have ℜ 𝝂 IR ​ ( T ) ≤ ℒ ​ W q ​ K q + 1 ​ ( ϵ q + T − q ) , ∀ T &gt; τ ϵ . \displaystyle\mathfrak{R}^{\rm IR}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}(T)\leq\mathcal{L}W^{q}K^{q+1}\left(\epsilon^{q}+T^{-q}\right)\ ,\qquad\forall T&gt;\tau^{\epsilon}\ . (61) Proof: See Appendix L.3 . Before providing the proof sketch of Theorem 9 , we provide some insights on the result. Note that in Theorem 9 , the arm selection regret scales as O ⁡ ( T − q ) O(T^{-q}) , which matches the regret bound of the PM-ETC-M algorithm in Theorem 4 , and improves upon the regret bound of the PM-UCB-M algorithm in Theorem 6 by an order of O ⁡ ( T − q 2 ) O(T^{-\frac{q}{2}}) . This observation stems from the fact that the CIRT algorithm inherits the better features of the PM-ETC-M and the PM-UCB-M algorithms. On the one hand, as opposed to the PM-ETC-M algorithm, which assumes knowing instance-dependent parameters through the length of its exploration phase, CIRT’s interval refinement phase is purely data-adaptive, requiring no instance-dependence. The overarching idea here is to use PM-UCB-M’s adaptive confidence set construction to guide the estimation routine to form a reliable estimate of the optimal mixing coefficient. On the other hand, from Theorem 6 , we observe that PM-UCB-M’s rationale of forming estimates in each round and subsequently tracking these estimates may hurt the arm selection regret, since, while more refined estimates of the mixing coefficients should be helpful to the algorithm, it may not be required after a certain point where we have reached the desired estimation accuracy. The CIRT algorithm identifies this drawback and commits to an estimate that it forms from the interval refinement phase. We note that the first term in the regret, i.e., the regret contribution from discretizing the mixing-coefficient grid, is driven entirely by the user’s choice of accuracy level, ϵ \epsilon . A tighter grid (smaller ϵ \epsilon ) directly reduces this error term but also weakens the regret guarantee. Because the user specifies ϵ \epsilon upfront, CIRT lets practitioners calibrate exactly how much estimation regret they are willing to accept in exchange for faster runtime. In contrast, horizon-aware schemes can tie their discretization levels to the time horizon T T , often forcing finer discretization (and higher cost) than strictly needed. When limited compute resources or rapid, anytime deployment are priorities—and a modest, user-controlled estimation regret is acceptable — CIRT presents a clear practical advantage. Additionally, note that the statement in Theorem 9 holds for T &gt; τ ϵ T&gt;\tau^{\epsilon} , where τ ϵ \tau^{\epsilon} is a stochastic quantity which is finite on average. It can be readily verified using Markov’s inequality that the probabilistic guarantee in Theorem 9 holds in the asymptotic limit of the horizon T T . Proof sketch of Theorem 9 . We first decompose the regret into an estimation regret component and an arm selection regret component. A bound on the arm selection regret follows the same arguments as Theorems 5 and 6 . The fundamental analytical crux appears in bounding the second term, i.e., quantifying the gap between the utility evaluated at the discrete optimal solution and CIRT’s arm selection fractions. A sublinear regret crucially hinges on the premise that the optimal solution is contained within the interval obtained after the last phase at the end of the interval refinement step, which holds due to Lemma 5 . The regret bound in Theorem 9 is conditioned on the event that this indeed holds, which is the source of the probabilistic guarantee on CIRT’s regret bound. Subsequently, the analysis proceeds by quantifying the sample complexity required for the desired estimation accuracy ϵ \epsilon , followed by upper bounding the regret in the tracking phase incurred by the under-sampling routine. The finiteness of the average sample complexity for a sufficiently accurate estimation is an immediate consequence of Lemma 7 . Finally, the analysis concludes by upper-bounding the sampling estimation error in the tracking phase. While the analysis is analogous to upper-bounding the sampling estimation error of the PM-UCB-M algorithm in Theorem 6 , there are subtle differences due to the order of explicit exploration being sublinear. For further details, we refer to Appendix L.3 . 6 Empirical Evaluations In this section, we provide empirical evaluations of the horizon-dependent algorithms and the anytime algorithm, namely PM-ETC-M, PM-UCB-M, CE-UCB-M, and CIRT algorithms. First, in Section 6.1 , we provide empirical evaluations for the horizon-dependent algorithms. We begin by empirically validating our claim (in Theorem 3 ) that for strictly concave utilities, the PM may be maximized by an optimal mixture. Subsequently, we empirically investigate two distinct aspects: (1) the scaling of the (average) regret versus horizon, and (2) the impact of the discretization level ε \varepsilon on the regret. Next, we digress to the anytime algorithm in Section 6.2 , where we show (1) the scaling of the (average) regret versus horizon after the tracking step, and (2) showcase the computational advantage of the anytime algorithm against the fixed-discretization counterpart. Utilities and Bandit Models. We empirically evaluate the PM-centric algorithms for different PMs, specifically, for the Gini deviation (GD) with distortion function h ⁡ ( u ) = u ⁡ ( 1 − u ) h(u)=u(1-u) , Wang’s right-tail deviation (WRTD) with distortion function h ⁡ ( u ) = u − u h(u)=\sqrt{u}-u , and mean (expected value) with distortion function h ⁡ ( u ) = u h(u)=u . For the empirical evaluations in Section 6.1 (horizon-dependent algorithms) and in Section 6.2 (anytime algorithm), we focus on Bernoulli bandits with mean vectors 𝐩 ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] K \mathbf{p}\in[0,1]^{K} , in which case, the PM has a closed-form expression for the case of mixtures, given by V ⁡ ( 𝜶 , 𝔽 ) = ⟨ 𝜶 , 𝐩 ⟩ ​ ( 1 − ⟨ 𝜶 , 𝐩 ⟩ ) V(\boldsymbol{\alpha},\mathbb{F})\;=\;\langle\boldsymbol{\alpha},\mathbf{p}\rangle(1-\langle\boldsymbol{\alpha},\mathbf{p}\rangle) . Additionally, for horizon-dependent algorithms, we consider Gaussian bandit models, for which we do not have a closed form. 6.1 Horizon-dependent Algorithms We first show that when the optimal solution is a mixture, the resulting PM is strictly higher than those yielded by any single arm. Subsequently, we empirically investigate the regrets that PM-UCB-M and PM-ETC-M algorithms incur for the Bernoulli bandit for different horizons. In the next step, we move on to Gaussian bandits and investigate the regrets of CE-UCB-M and PM-ETC-M algorithms for different horizons. Finally, we perform an ablation study to assess the impact of the discretization level ε \varepsilon on regret. Mixture Optimality. We investigate the observations established in Theorems 2 and 3 for the optimality of solitary policies for convex distortion functions and the optimality of mixture policies for strictly concave ones. For strictly concave cases, we consider GD and WRTD PMs, and for the convex case, we consider the mean PM. To empirically showcase the optimality of mixtures, for a 3 3 -armed bandit instance, we evaluate the PM achieved by PM-UCB-M against the utility achieved by solitary arm baselines for each arm indexed by { 0 , 1 , 2 } \{0,1,2\} . Each experiment is averaged over 100 100 independent trials. In Figures 2(a) and 2(b) , we observe that after a sufficiently large horizon, for GD and WRTD, the PM achieved by PM-UCB-M is strictly higher than those of the solitary arm baselines, which corroborates the finding in Theorem 3 . Furthermore, for the mean value, in Figure 2(c) we observe that the utility is maximized by arm 1 1 , and PM-UCB-M almost yields a lower PM, which aligns with the property foreseen by Theorem 2 . Furthermore, these observations establish the important property that the PM-UCB-M algorithm automatically tracks the optimal mixture or the optimal solitary arm, depending on the underlying setting. (a) GD. (b) WRTD. (c) mean Figure 2 : Empirical utility values for GD, WRTD, and mean for PM-UCB-M algorithm. Regret versus Horizon (Bernoulli Bandits). Next, we evaluate the regret incurred by the PM-ETC-M and PM-UCB-M algorithms for various horizon values T T . For performance comparison, we adopt the uniform sampling as the baseline. The uniform sampling strategy allocates the same allocation resource for each arm, i.e., each arm is sampled ⌊ T K ⌋ \big\lfloor\frac{T}{K}\big\rfloor times, with the remaining instants uniformly randomly assigned to arms. For the PM-ETC-M algorithm, note that T &gt; N ⁡ ( ε ) T&gt;N(\varepsilon) is a necessary condition. However, for the utilities and bandit instances in consideration, N ⁡ ( ε ) N(\varepsilon) may potentially be of the order O ⁡ ( T ) O(T) , and hence, providing experiments with a larger horizon is computationally prohibitive. Instead, we fix the horizon T T within an implementable range. If N ⁡ ( ε ) &gt; T 2 N(\varepsilon)&gt;\frac{T}{2} , we set N ⁡ ( ε ) = 1 2 ​ K ​ ε ​ T N(\varepsilon)=\frac{1}{2}K\varepsilon T . Furthermore, we choose ε \varepsilon according to values specified in Section 4 . All the experiments are averaged over 100 100 independent trials. As seen in Figures 3(b) , 3(d) , the uniform sampling strategy incurs a large regret in all our experiments. We observe that the regret of PM-ETC-M has a larger variance compared to PM-UCB-M. This is expected, as it commits to one mixing coefficient while PM-UCB-M continuously uses the information acquired from the rewards to choose a mixing coefficient. Therefore, if PM-ETC-M commits to an optimal mixing coefficient in one run, the resulting regret will be low. However, if it commits a suboptimal mixing coefficient, the regret will be higher. As PM-UCB-M does not commit to a mixing coefficient and instead continuously uses the new information to have a better estimate for the mixing coefficient, the resulting regret varies less. (d) Horizon-dependent algorithm compared to uniform sampling for WRTD Figure 3 : Performance comparison of PM-ETC-M and PM-UCB-M algorithms on 2-arm Bernoulli bandits under (top) GD and (bottom) WRTD PMs, with and without a uniform-sampling baseline. Error bars denote one standard error over repeated trials. Regret versus Horizon (Gaussian Bandits) We also empirically evaluate CE-UCB-M and PM-ETC-M algorithms for the Gaussian Bandit model. We assess the regrets of two convex (mean) and concave (GD) models. We implement CE-UCB-M since it has computational advantages over PM-UCB-M, while achieving the same regret bounds. The Gaussian distributions have variance 1 1 and mean values 1 , 3 1,3 and 5 5 . Our choice of ε \varepsilon depends on β \beta . For the mean, the optimal solution is the arm that has the highest mean 5 5 , i.e., the solution lies on the boundaries of Δ ε K − 1 \Delta^{K-1}_{\varepsilon} . By Case 1 analyzed in G , which does not require strict concavity of the distortion function, it follows that β = 1 \beta=1 . For GD, the optimal solution might be a mixture of arm distributions. From Lemma 4 , it is known that β ∈ { 1 , 2 } \beta\in\{1,2\} for GD. In the absence of knowledge about the optimal policy, we set β = 2 \beta=2 , to obtain conservative scaling for δ 12 ​ ( ε ) \delta_{12}(\varepsilon) . As seen in Figures 4(a) and 4(b) , both algorithms achieve sublinear regret for the mean and GD. Furthermore, these figures show that for GD, PM-ETC-M incurs less regret, whereas for the mean, CE-UCB-M incurs less regret. (a) Scaling of regret for GD (b) Scaling of regret for mean. Impact of Discretization Resolution. For our empirical experiments, we have chosen the smallest discretization parameter ε \varepsilon that still satisfies these guarantees required for the regret bounds in Section 4 . While a smaller ε \varepsilon brings the solution closer to optimality, it also increases computational cost. By selecting the minimal ε \varepsilon that preserves our regret bounds, we strike a balance: as the horizon T T grows, regret decreases without incurring unnecessary computation. For GD, we empirically show how the regret upper bounds would scale for different ε \varepsilon values in Figures 6(a) and 6(b) . We consider the distributions in which the optimal solution will not lie on the boundary points, hence, β = 2 \beta=2 . We plotted the regret bounds for ε \varepsilon that comply with T &gt; T ⁡ ( ε ) T&gt;T(\varepsilon) requirement for Theorem 7 in Figure 6(a) and T &gt; N ⁡ ( ε ) T&gt;N(\varepsilon) requirement for Theorem 5 in Figure 6(b) . We observe that as ε \varepsilon increases, the regret bounds worsen as the estimate for the mixing coefficient becomes less accurate. (a) PM-UCB-M (b) PM-ETC-M Figure 6 : Empirical evaluations of regret upper-bound of horizon-dependent algorithms for GD 6.2 Anytime Algorithm The anytime algorithm has two main phases: the interval refinement phase and the tracking phase. We focus on evaluating the algorithm’s behavior once the tracking phase starts. This is due to the fact that before the refinement phase starts, the discretization is not on the level that the user specified. Due to this, our regret guarantee is also after the tracking phase of the algorithm starts. To this end, we first apply the anytime algorithm to the 2 2 -arm Bernoulli with horizon of T = 3 ⋅ 10 6 T=3\cdot 10^{6} , discretization interval length of ϵ ∈ { 0.12 , 0.06 , 0.03 } \epsilon\in\{0.12,0.06,0.03\} , discretization number A = 4 A=4 , and target probability of error δ = 0.05 \delta=0.05 . We perform the experiment 100 times. We observe that for larger ϵ \epsilon , the tracking phase starts far earlier. However, the regret incurred is much larger, which is expected as the estimation regret for a given ϵ \epsilon is a constant. (a) ε = 0.03 \varepsilon=0.03 (b) ε = 0.06 \varepsilon=0.06 (c) ε = 0.12 \varepsilon=0.12 Figure 7 : Regret of the anytime algorithm versus horizon T T for various ε \varepsilon values. An important feature of the anytime algorithm is the computational time. Different from horizon-dependent algorithms, where we encountered a O ⁡ ( 1 ε K ) O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{K}}) -dimensional discrete optimization problem, with the anytime algorithm, we only have a O ⁡ ( 1 A K ) O(\frac{1}{A^{K}}) -dimensional discrete optimization problem where A A is a user specified constant that does not scale with the horizon. Therefore, to show the benefit of adaptive discretization, we propose the following experimental setup. First, for performance comparisons, we design a reasonable baseline algorithm. Since the main characteristic of our approach is the adaptive discretization, we construct a variation of the algorithm with fixed discretization for comparison. Specifically, we adapt PM-UCB-M into an anytime setting by setting ε = ϵ / A \varepsilon=\epsilon/A and modifying the sampling rule according to the logic used in the CIRT algorithm. The result is a fixed-discretization, anytime-compatible algorithm. To evaluate and compare computational efficiencies of both algorithms, we define a set of thresholds for regret, namely, { 10 − 2 , 10 − 3 , 10 − 4 , 10 − 5 , 10 − 6 } \{10^{-2},10^{-3},10^{-4},10^{-5},10^{-6}\} , ϵ = 0.06 \epsilon=0.06 and A = 4 A=4 . Each algorithm is initialized run for increasing horizons until the observed regret falls below the specified threshold. We then record the computation time required to reach this point. For each threshold, we run the experiments 100 100 times, and report the average of the computational time. As shown in Figure 8 , CIRT achieves substantially lower computation time than its fixed-discretization variation, especially for smaller regret thresholds. This observation is expected as computational complexity per time step in CIRT scales with A A , whereas in the fixed-discretization algorithm it scales with A / ϵ A/\epsilon , which is significantly larger. Figure 8 : Computation times for the anytime algorithm and the fixed discretization algorithm. 7 Application to Risk-sensitive Bandits Risk-sensitive bandits [ 30 , 23 , 15 , 14 , 16 , 17 , 18 , 20 , 31 , 32 ] has received notable attention in recent years. In such settings, instead of quantifying the quality of an arm through its mean value, a risk measure quantifies the quality of an arm by evaluating aspects of its reward distribution beyond the mean, often emphasizing tail behavior or variability to better capture application-specific preferences. Two main perspectives exist in the risk-sensitive bandit literature. One line of work focuses on risk-averse objectives, in which the regret is defined relative to the risk-minimizing arm [ 23 , 14 ] , a setting typically adopted when both losses and gains are modeled through rewards. In contrast, in another line of investigation [ 33 , 17 , 15 , 19 ] risk-seeking objectives are explored, in which the rewards primarily model gains, motivating the learner to favor highly volatile options over more stable choices. While much of the literature has focused on specific risk measures such as CVaR [ 16 , 17 ] , there have also been efforts to develop unified frameworks encompassing broader classes of risk measures [ 34 , 14 , 23 ] . For example, distortion risk measures (DRMs) – which are equivalent to spectral risk measures – form a rich family that includes CVaR, PHT, dual power, and quadratic risk measures. DRMs have been investigated in the context of risk-sensitive bandits in [ 19 , 23 ] . Importantly, our definition of a PM in ( 1 ) specializes to a DRM if the distortion function satisfies h ⁡ ( 1 ) = 1 h(1)=1 . In this section, we will discuss the application of the PM-centric framework in the context of risk-sensitivity, contrasting the regret guarantees of our proposed algorithms with the existing literature. We remark that the PM-centric framework specializes to the risk-seeking viewpoint, which has been investigated in earlier studies [ 33 , 17 , 19 ] . Hence, our performance comparisons have been provided with respect to these studies. The study in [ 17 ] investigates regret minimization in stochastic bandits, where regret of an arm is defined with respect to the arm having the maximal CVaR and provides a problem-independent bound of the order O ⁡ ( 1 / T ) O(1/\sqrt{T}) . The investigation in [ 33 ] provides a Thompson sampling counterpart of the UCB-based algorithm proposed in [ 17 ] for CVaR bandits, showcasing an empirical improvement over the UCB-based strategy. Finally, [ 19 ] extends the class of risk measures from CVaR to DRMs with Hölder-continuous utilities with exponent α \alpha , providing an order of O ~ ​ ( T − α 2 ) \widetilde{O}(T^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}) problem-independent regret. Performance Comparison. Next, we provide detailed comparisons of the settings and reported regrets for CVaR bandits and DRMs [ 17 , 33 , 19 ] . Furthermore, for CVaR bandits, we also compare against the UCB-based algorithm proposed for the EDPM framework in [ 14 ] . Even though the focus of [ 14 ] is on risk aversion, for CVaR, a reward-based definition is considered, which translates to the risk-seeking objective. Before providing performance comparisons, we remark that the existing investigations on risk-sensitive bandits focus on solitary arm optimality. To recover the counterpart regrets of the PM-centric algorithms, we evaluate them when they generate solitary arm decisions. This can be controlled by properly selecting our simplex discretization scheme. Specifically, by setting ε = 1 \varepsilon=1 , we can report solitary-arm policies and regrets for PM-ETC-M. Consequently, we have δ 01 = 0 \delta_{01}=0 , and hence we only have the arm selection regret ℜ ¯ 𝝂 , 1 E ​ ( T ) \bar{\mathfrak{R}}_{\boldsymbol{\nu},1}^{\rm E}(T) . For the PM-UCB-M algorithm, it can be readily verified that β = 1 \beta=1 , since the PM-maximizing arm is solitary, and we report regrets with β = 1 \beta=1 . • CVaR: The existing regret bounds for CVaR are restricted to distributions with bounded supports [ 17 , 33 , 14 ] . With this assumption, the best known regret bound is O ⁡ ( 1 T ​ log ⁡ T ) O(\frac{1}{T}\log T) . Furthermore, for a loss-based definition of CVaR, [ 23 ] reports a O ⁡ ( 1 T ​ log ⁡ T ) O(\sqrt{\frac{1}{T}\log T}) regret bound for sub-Gaussian distributions. Our PM-centric framework generalizes the regret bound for reward-based CVaR to sub-Gaussian distributions. PM-ETC-M achieves a regret bound of the order O ⁡ ( 1 T ​ log ⁡ T ) O(\frac{1}{T}\log T) , which is achieved by setting ε = 1 \varepsilon=1 and noting that q = 1 q=1 for CVaR. The PM-UCB-M algorithm achieves a regret bound of the order O ⁡ ( ( 1 T ​ log ⁡ T ) κ ) O((\frac{1}{T}\log T)^{\kappa}) , assuming that β \beta exists, where κ \kappa and β \beta are defined in ( 36 ) and ( 30 ), respectively. Hence, for CVaR bandits, the PM-centric framework generalizes to sub-Gaussian distributions (from bounded support) and matches the best known regret reported in the literature. • DRMs: The existing studies of DRMs are reported in [ 19 ] and [ 23 ] , where the former adopts the risk-seeking objective while the latter adopts the risk-averse perspective, and reports a regret bound of O ⁡ ( T − q 2 ​ ( log ⁡ T ) q 2 ) O(T^{-\frac{q}{2}}(\log T)^{\frac{q}{2}}) . The study in [ 19 ] reports a problem-independent regret bound of the order O ⁡ ( T − q 2 ​ ( log ⁡ T ) q 2 ) O(T^{-\frac{q}{2}}(\log T)^{\frac{q}{2}}) for distributions with bounded supports. In contrast, the PM-centric framework generalizes to sub-Gaussian distributions. Furthermore, the PM-ETC-M algorithm outperforms the existing baseline in [ 19 ] , achieving a regret of the order O ⁡ ( ( 1 T ​ log ⁡ T ) q ) O((\frac{1}{T}\log T)^{q}) when we set ε = 1 \varepsilon=1 . On the other hand, the PM-UCB-M admits a regret bound of order O ⁡ ( ( 1 T ​ log ⁡ T ) q ​ κ ) O((\frac{1}{T}\log T)^{q\kappa}) , assuming that β \beta exists. It is noteworthy that β \beta has been shown to exist for strictly concave distortion functions in Lemma 4 , which includes a broad range of DRMs such as PHT, quadratic, and dual power risk measures. To summarize, for DRMs, the PM-centric framework exhibits two advantages: (1) it generalizes to sub-Gaussian distributions (from bounded support) for measures such as quadratic and dual power, and (2) it improves upon the best known regret guarantees for DRMs for investigations into the risk-seeking objective. 8 Concluding Remarks In this paper, we have designed a novel preference-centric framework for viewing and analyzing bandit problems. It shifts bandit decisions from purely utilitarian and their rewards being defined as expected arm returns to a more general notion of reward that is sensitive not only to distributions’ first moments, but also recognizes the events encoded in the distribution tails. For this purpose, we have designed the notion of preference metric (PM) as a generalization of the canonical objective, which encapsulates a wide variety of finer distributional properties through distorted functionals of the arms’ cumulative distribution functions (CDFs). Adopting such a notion, besides facilitating decisions that are more sensitive to the entire probability model, also leads to a different way of thinking and designing bandit algorithms. Notably, an important observation in the PM-centric setting is that if the distortion function is strictly concave, there always exist bandit instances for which the PM will be optimized only by an arm selection policy that carefully selects a mixture of arms over time according to a well-defined mixture. This is in sharp contrast to the conventional approaches in bandits, which have the objective of identifying and pursuing a single best arm. Motivated by this observation, we have designed both horizon-dependent (PM-ETC-M, PM-UCB-M, and CE-UCB-M) algorithms and an anytime algorithm (CIRT). The key components in these algorithms that facilitate generalizing to mixture optimality are: (i) an estimation routine that forms estimates of the mixing coefficients based on CDF estimates computed from the accrued rewards, and, (ii) a tracking mechanism that navigates the arm selection fractions to match the estimated mixtures. We have analyzed the regret of the proposed algorithms for a wide range of PMs based on utility-specific parameters, which guide in selecting an algorithm given a specific choice of PM and the availability of instance-dependent information. Furthermore, we have experimentally evaluated several facets of the proposed algorithms, including their regret performance, superiority compared to solitary arm policies, and computational complexity. This investigation lays the foundation for viewing bandit problems through the lens of PMs and opens several avenues for future research. First, designing minimax and instance-dependent lower bounds for PMs with mixture optimality is a promising direction. The canonical approach of applying the change-of-measures argument to two different bandit instances with distinct optimal arms does not work, since PMs may exhibit mixture optimality. Second, our results rely on the exponential convergence of the CDF estimates in the 1 1 -Wasserstein metric, which follows from the sub-Gaussianity of the class of bandits we investigate. Extending these results to heavy-tailed distributions is an open problem and would necessitate designing distribution confidence sequences for heavy-tailed distributions. Third, another important direction is to devise provably no-regret anytime algorithms beyond Bernoulli bandits. Finally, while we analyze PM-centric stochastic bandits, extending the PM-centric setting to correlated bandit classes, such as linear and causal bandits, has important applications. Organization of the Appendix The supplementary material consists of nine parts grouped in Appendices A - L . We begin by presenting the proof of Lemma 1 in Appendix A , Lemma 2 in Appendix B , and the proof of the finiteness of the Wasserstein-related constant W W in Appendix C . Then, in Appendix D , we provide the proofs for Theorem 2 , which shows solitary arm optimality for convex distortion functions, and Theorem 3 , which shows possible mixture optimality for strict concave distortion functions. Subsequently, in Appendix E , we provide an upper bound on the estimation regret in terms of the discretization parameter ε \varepsilon . Then, in Appendix F , we obtain Hölder exponents and constants for PMs reported in Table 3 . We continue with proof of Lemma 4 in Appendix G . Then, in Appendix H , we obtain gap parameter values β \beta and β ¯ {\bar{\beta}} for K − K- arm Bernoulli bandit model. Lastly, we have Appendices I - L to present the performance guarantees for the PM-ETC-M (Appendix I ), PM-UCB-M (Appendix J ), CE-UCB-M and (Appendix K ). and CIRT (Appendix L ) algorithms. (81) Appendix D Convex and Concave Preference Metrics D.1 Proof of Theorem 2 (Convex Distortion Functions) For an arm i ∈ [ K ] i\in[K] , we have U h ​ ( 𝔽 i ) \displaystyle U_{h}\left(\mathbb{F}_{i}\right) = ∫ − ∞ ∞ ( h ⁡ ( 1 − 𝔽 i ​ ( x ) ) − h ⁡ ( 1 ) ​ u ​ ( − x ) ) ​ 𝑑 x , \displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left(h\left(1-\mathbb{F}_{i}(x)\right)-h(1)u(-x)\right){\rm d}x\ , (82) where u ⁡ ( ⋅ ) u(\cdot) denotes the unit step function. Let us consider a convex combination of arm CDFs, ∑ i ∈ [ K ] α ⁡ ( i ) ​ 𝔽 i \sum_{i\in[K]}\alpha(i)\mathbb{F}_{i} where α ⁡ ( i ) ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] \alpha(i)\in[0,1] and ∑ i ∈ [ K ] α ⁡ ( i ) = 1 \sum_{i\in[K]}\alpha(i)=1 . From ( 88 ), we can conclude that for a PM with a convex distortion function, the optimal solution is a solitary arm. D.2 Proof of Theorem 3 (Concave Distortion Functions) The proof proceeds in two steps. First, we construct an appropriate bandit instance such that each arm has the same utility for a strictly concave distortion function. Secondly, we will show that the constructed bandit instance always admits mixtures optimality for strictly concave distortion functions. Constructing bandit model: Consider a K K -arm shifted exponential bandit model. Let each arm have CDF denoted by 𝔽 i \mathbb{F}_{i} . For c i , λ i &gt; 0 c_{i},\lambda_{i}&gt;0 ∀ i ∈ [ K ] \forall i\in[K] , we have 𝔽 i ​ ( x ) = { 1 − e − λ i ​ ( x − c i ) , if x ≥ c i 0 , otherwise . \displaystyle\mathbb{F}_{i}(x)=\begin{cases}1-e^{-\lambda_{i}(x-c_{i})}\ ,\quad&amp;\text{if}\quad x\geq c_{i}\\ 0\ ,\qquad\qquad\qquad&amp;\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\ . (89) We start by expressing the utility function in terms of the parameters of distributions for arm i ∈ [ K ] i\in[K] . Denoting by u ⁡ ( ⋅ ) u(\cdot) the unit step function, we have U h ​ ( 𝔽 i ) \displaystyle U_{h}(\mathbb{F}_{i}) = ∫ − ∞ ∞ ( h ⁡ ( 1 − 𝔽 i ​ ( x ) ) − h ⁡ ( 1 ) ​ u ​ ( − x ) ) ​ 𝑑 x \displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left(h\left(1-\mathbb{F}_{i}(x)\right)-h(1)u(-x)\right){\rm d}x (90) = ∫ 0 ∞ h ⁡ ( 1 − 𝔽 i ​ ( x ) ) ​ 𝑑 x \displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}h\left(1-\mathbb{F}_{i}(x)\right){\rm d}x (91) = ∫ 0 c i h ⁡ ( 1 ) ​ 𝑑 x + ∫ c i ∞ h ⁡ ( 1 − 𝔽 i ​ ( x ) ) ​ 𝑑 x \displaystyle=\int_{0}^{c_{i}}h\left(1\right){\rm d}x+\int_{c_{i}}^{\infty}h\left(1-\mathbb{F}_{i}(x)\right){\rm d}x (92) = c i ​ h ​ ( 1 ) + ∫ c i ∞ h ⁡ ( e − λ i ​ ( x − c i ) ) ​ 𝑑 x \displaystyle=c_{i}h\left(1\right)+\int_{c_{i}}^{\infty}h\left(e^{-\lambda_{i}(x-c_{i})}\right){\rm d}x (93) = c i ​ h ​ ( 1 ) + ∫ 0 ∞ h ⁡ ( e − λ i ​ x ) ​ 𝑑 x \displaystyle=c_{i}h\left(1\right)+\int_{0}^{\infty}h\left(e^{-\lambda_{i}x}\right){\rm d}x (94) In the case when h ⁡ ( 1 ) = 0 h(1)=0 , choosing different c i c_{i} for each arm i i and same λ i \lambda_{i} results in U h ​ ( 𝔽 i ) = U h ​ ( 𝔽 j ) U_{h}(\mathbb{F}_{i})=U_{h}(\mathbb{F}_{j}) , ∀ i , j ∈ [ K ] \forall i,j\in[K] . In contrast, if h ⁡ ( 1 ) ≠ 0 h(1)\neq 0 , we can always construct ( c i , λ i ) (c_{i},\lambda_{i}) pairs such that U h ​ ( 𝔽 i ) = U h ​ ( 𝔽 j ) U_{h}(\mathbb{F}_{i})=U_{h}(\mathbb{F}_{j}) , ∀ i , j ∈ [ K ] \forall i,j\in[K] . Hence, we have designed bandit instances such that for all arms i ∈ [ K ] i\in[K] , U h ​ ( 𝔽 i ) = U h ​ ( 𝔽 j ) U_{h}(\mathbb{F}_{i})=U_{h}(\mathbb{F}_{j}) . Mixtures optimality: Let us choose 𝜶 ∈ int ⁡ ( Δ K − 1 ) \boldsymbol{\alpha}\in{\rm int}(\Delta^{K-1}) , where int ⁡ ( 𝒮 ) {\rm int}(\mathcal{S}) denotes the interior of a compact set 𝒮 \mathcal{S} . From ( 99 ), it is observed that there is no solitary arm that is better than the optimal mixture 𝜶 \boldsymbol{\alpha} . Appendix E Upper Bound on Estimation Regret The following lemma upper bounds the estimation regret for all algorithms, PM-ETC-M, PM-UCB-M, CE-UCB-M, and CIRT. It will be used in the analyses of Theorems 4 , 6 , and 9 . (117) Appendix F Hölder Exponents and Constants In this section, we will show the Hölder exponent and constant values for some PM functions. For the analyses of Hölder exponents and constants, we leverage the following lemma, the proof of which can be found in [ 23 , Lemma 2] . Lemma 9 Consider random variables X X and Y Y with CDFs F X F_{X} and F Y F_{Y} , respectively. Then, ‖ F X − F Y ‖ W = sup ‖ f ‖ L ≤ 1 | 𝔼 ( f ( X ) − 𝔼 ( f ( Y ) ) | = ∫ − ∞ ∞ | F X ( s ) − F Y ( s ) | d s = ∫ 0 1 | F X − 1 ( β ) − F Y − 1 ( β ) | d β . \left\lVert F_{X}-F_{Y}\right\rVert_{\rm{W}}=\sup_{\|f\|_{L}\leq 1}\Big|{\mathbb{E}}(f(X)-{\mathbb{E}}(f(Y))\Big|=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|F_{X}(s)-F_{Y}(s)|\mathrm{d}s=\int_{0}^{1}|F_{X}^{-1}(\beta)-F_{Y}^{-1}(\beta)|\mathrm{d}\beta\ . (118) The results provided in this section are summarized in Table 4 . In order to unify some lemmas for presenting the Hölder constants and exponents, we define the following two categories of distortion functions. Definition 2 (Category 1 1 PMs) We define the category 1 1 of PMs as the one induced by a distortion function of the form h ⁡ ( u ) = C 1 ​ u m − C 2 ​ u 2 ​ m h(u)=C_{1}u^{m}-C_{2}u^{2m} , where C 1 ≥ C 2 &gt; 0 C_{1}\geq C_{2}&gt;0 and m ∈ ( 0 , 1 ] m\in(0,1] . The PMs under Category 1 1 include Gini deviation and Wang’s Right-tail deviation. Definition 3 (Category 2 2 PMs) We define the category 2 2 of PMs as the one induced by a distortion function of the form h ⁡ ( u ) = C ​ min ⁡ { u , ( 1 − u ) } h(u)=C\min\{u,(1-u)\} , where C ∈ ℝ + C\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+} . Some examples of Category 2 2 PMs include mean-median deviation and the inter-ES range ( c = 0.5 c=0.5 ) ( IER 0.5 {\rm IER}_{0.5} ). (169) Let us assume that H ⁡ ( 𝜶 ⋆ ) H(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\star}) is negative definite. By Taylor’s theorem around 𝐚 ( 1 ) \mathbf{a}^{(1)} , we have U ⁡ ( 𝐚 ( 2 ) ) = U ⁡ ( 𝐚 ( 1 ) ) + ∇ U ​ ( 𝐚 ( 1 ) ) ⋅ ( 𝐚 ( 2 ) − 𝐚 ( 1 ) ) + 1 2 ​ ( 𝐚 ( 2 ) − 𝐚 ( 1 ) ) T ​ H ​ ( 𝐚 ( 1 ) ) ​ ( 𝐚 ( 2 ) − 𝐚 ( 1 ) ) + O ⁡ ( ‖ 𝐚 ( 2 ) − 𝐚 ( 1 ) ‖ 3 ) . \displaystyle U(\mathbf{a}^{(2)})=U(\mathbf{a}^{(1)})+\nabla U(\mathbf{a}^{(1)})\cdot(\mathbf{a}^{(2)}-\mathbf{a}^{(1)})+\frac{1}{2}(\mathbf{a}^{(2)}-\mathbf{a}^{(1)})^{T}H(\mathbf{a}^{(1)})(\mathbf{a}^{(2)}-\mathbf{a}^{(1)})+O(||\mathbf{a}^{(2)}-\mathbf{a}^{(1)}||^{3})\ . (170) We define v ε ≜ 𝐚 ( 2 ) − 𝐚 ( 1 ) v_{\varepsilon}\triangleq\mathbf{a}^{(2)}-\mathbf{a}^{(1)} . Appendix H β ¯ {\bar{\beta}} Analysis for Bernoulli Bandits In this section, we analyze the parameter β \beta for various PMs and the K K -arm Bernoulli bandit model with mean values 𝐩 = ( p ⁡ ( 1 ) , ⋯ , p ⁡ ( K ) ) \mathbf{p}=(p(1),\cdots,p(K)) . I.3 Upper Bound on the PM-ETC-M Sampling Estimation Error A 3 ​ ( T ) A_{3}(T) Next, we analyze A 3 ​ ( T ) A_{3}(T) , which captures the sampling estimation error. We have A 3 ​ ( T ) \displaystyle A_{3}(T) = 𝔼 𝝂 E ​ [ U h ​ ( ∑ i ∈ [ K ] a N ⁡ ( ε ) E ​ ( i ) ​ 𝔽 i ) − U h ​ ( ∑ i ∈ [ K ] τ T E ​ ( i ) T ​ 𝔽 i ) ] \displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}^{\rm E}\left[U_{h}\left(\sum_{i\in[K]}a^{\rm E}_{N(\varepsilon)}(i)\mathbb{F}_{i}\right)-U_{h}\left(\sum_{i\in[K]}\frac{\tau_{T}^{\rm E}(i)}{T}\mathbb{F}_{i}\right)\right] (277) ≤ ℒ ​ 𝔼 𝝂 E ​ [ ‖ 𝐚 N ⁡ ( ε ) E − 𝝉 T E T ‖ 1 q ​ W q ] \displaystyle\leq\mathcal{L}{\mathbb{E}}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}^{\rm E}\left[\left\lVert\mathbf{a}^{\rm E}_{N(\varepsilon)}-\frac{\boldsymbol{\tau}_{T}^{\rm E}}{T}\right\rVert_{1}^{q}W^{q}\right] (278) ≤ ℒ ​ K ​ W q ​ 𝔼 𝝂 E ​ [ max i ∈ [ K ] ⁡ | a N ⁡ ( ε ) E ​ ( i ) − τ t E ​ ( i ) T | q ] , \displaystyle\leq\mathcal{L}KW^{q}{\mathbb{E}}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}^{\rm E}\bigg[\max_{i\in[K]}\left|a^{\rm E}_{N(\varepsilon)}(i)-\frac{\tau_{t}^{\rm E}(i)}{T}\right|^{q}\bigg], (279) where ( 278 ) follows from the Hölder continuity of the utility stated in ( 10 ) and recalling the definition of W W stated in ( 9 ). We need to find an upper bound on the estimation error due to the policy’s sampling proportions. J.3 Upper Bound on the Sampling Estimation Error Finally, we will bound B 22 ​ ( T ) B_{22}(T) . The key idea in upper bounding the sampling estimation error is to show that after a certain instant, the PM-UCB-M algorithm outputs an estimate 𝜶 t U \boldsymbol{\alpha}_{t}^{\rm U} of the mixing coefficients that belongs to the set of discrete optimal mixtures with a very high probability. Subsequently, we show that the undersampling routine of the PM-UCB-M algorithm ensures that once it has identified a correct optimal mixture, it navigates its sampling proportions to match the estimated coefficient. Let us denote the vector of arm sampling fractions by 𝝉 T U ≜ [ τ T U ​ ( 1 ) , ⋯ , τ T U ​ ( K ) ] ⊤ \boldsymbol{\tau}_{T}^{\rm U}\triangleq[\tau_{T}^{\rm U}(1),\cdots,\tau_{T}^{\rm U}(K)]^{\top} . Lemma 20 For event ℰ 3 , T \mathcal{E}_{3,T} , we have ℙ 𝝂 U ​ ( ℰ 3 , T ) ≤ T ⁡ ( ( 2 T 2 + 1 ) K − 1 ) . \displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}^{\rm U}\left(\mathcal{E}_{3,T}\right)\leq T\left(\left(\frac{2}{T^{2}}+1\right)^{K}-1\right)\ . (385) Proof: Note that ℙ 𝝂 U ( ∃ t &gt; T 0 ( ε ) : OPT ~ ε , t ≠ OPT ε ) \displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}^{\rm U}\Big(\exists t&gt;T_{0}(\varepsilon):\widetilde{\rm OPT}_{\varepsilon,t}\neq{\rm OPT}_{\varepsilon}\Big) ≤ ∑ t = T 0 ​ ( ε ) + 1 T ℙ ⁡ ( OPT ~ ε , t ≠ OPT ε ) \displaystyle\leq\sum_{t=T_{0}(\varepsilon)+1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left(\widetilde{\rm OPT}_{\varepsilon,t}\neq{\rm OPT}_{\varepsilon}\right) (386) ≤ ( 365 ) ∑ t = T 0 ​ ( ε ) + 1 T ( ( 2 T 2 + 1 ) K − 1 ) \displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:UCB_UBh1}}}{{\leq}}\sum_{t=T_{0}(\varepsilon)+1}^{T}\left(\left(\frac{2}{T^{2}}+1\right)^{K}-1\right) + ℙ 𝝂 U ​ ( 𝐚 t U ∉ OPT ε | ℰ o , t ) \displaystyle\qquad+{\mathbb{P}}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}^{\rm U}\Big(\mathbf{a}^{\rm U}_{t}\notin{\rm OPT}_{\varepsilon}\;|\;\mathcal{E}_{o,t}\Big) (387) ≤ ( 382 ) T ⁡ ( ( 2 T 2 + 1 ) K − 1 ) . \displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:UCB_UB10}}}{{\leq}}T\left(\left(\frac{2}{T^{2}}+1\right)^{K}-1\right)\ . (388) First, thanks to the discretization, all arms are sampled at least once, according to under-sampling. Let us assume that after the explicit exploration, an arm is never under sampled. τ t ​ ( i ) = ρ 4 ​ T ​ ϵ . \displaystyle\tau_{t}(i)=\frac{\rho}{4}T\epsilon\ . (389) If an arm is not under sampled that would mean τ t ​ ( i ) ≥ t ​ a t U ​ ( i ) ⟹ ρ 4 ​ T ​ ϵ ​ t − 1 ≥ a t U ​ ( i ) ≥ ε 2 ⟹ ρ 2 \displaystyle\tau_{t}(i)\geq ta_{t}^{U}(i)\Longrightarrow\frac{\rho}{4}T\epsilon t^{-1}\geq a_{t}^{U}(i)\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\Longrightarrow\frac{\rho}{2} ≥ t T , \displaystyle\geq\frac{t}{T}\ , (390) where right hand side follows from ( 389 ) and ( 390 ), and from the definition of discretization. For t &gt; ρ ​ T 2 t&gt;\frac{\rho T}{2} , this inequality does not hold. Therefore, no matter which mixing coefficient is chosen, if an arm is not sampled after the explicit exploration, it becomes under sampled at the latest at the time instant ρ ​ T 2 \frac{\rho T}{2} which means after that time instant it will be sampled according to under-sampling rule. Next, we will show that under the event ℰ ¯ 3 , T \bar{\mathcal{E}}_{3,T} , i.e., when the PM-UCB-M algorithm only selects a mixture distribution from the set of optimal mixtures, the PM-UCB-M arm selection routine, using the under-sampling procedure, eventually converges to an optimal mixture distribution. This is captured in the following lemma. Prior to stating the lemma, note that under the event ℰ ¯ 3 , T \bar{\mathcal{E}}_{3,T} , the set of estimated mixing coefficients is the same in each iteration between T 0 ​ ( ε ) T_{0}(\varepsilon) and T T . Hence, the PM-UCB-M algorithm aims to track one of these optimal mixing coefficients, which we denote by 𝐚 ( 1 ) ∈ OPT ε \mathbf{a}^{(1)}\in{\rm OPT}_{\varepsilon} . Lemma 21 Under the event ℰ ¯ 3 , T \bar{\mathcal{E}}_{3,T} , there exists a time instant T ⁡ ( ε ) &lt; + ∞ T(\varepsilon)&lt;+\infty , and 𝐚 ( 1 ) ∈ OPT ε \mathbf{a}^{(1)}\in{\rm OPT}_{\varepsilon} such that we have | τ t U ​ ( i ) t − a ( 1 ) ​ ( i ) | &lt; K t ∀ t ≥ T ⁡ ( ε ) . \displaystyle\left|\frac{\tau_{t}^{\rm U}(i)}{t}-a^{(1)}(i)\right|&lt;\frac{K}{t}\quad\forall t\geq T(\varepsilon)\ . (391) Proof: We begin by defining a set of over-sampled arms as follows. We define the set 𝒪 t \mathcal{O}_{t} as 𝒪 t ≜ { i ∈ [ K ] : τ t U ​ ( i ) t &gt; a ( 1 ) ​ ( i ) + 1 t } . \displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{t}\;\triangleq\;\left\{i\in[K]:\frac{\tau_{t}^{\rm U}(i)}{t}&gt;a^{(1)}(i)+\frac{1}{t}\right\}\ . (392) We will leverage Lemma 22 , in which we show that there exists a finite time instant, which we call T ⁡ ( ε ) T(\varepsilon) , such that for all t &gt; T ⁡ ( ε ) t&gt;T(\varepsilon) the set of over-sampled arms is empty . Specifically, leveraging Lemma 22 we obtain τ t U ​ ( i ) t − a ( 1 ) ​ ( i ) ≤ 1 t ∀ t ≥ T ⁡ ( ε ) , \displaystyle\frac{\tau_{t}^{\rm U}(i)}{t}-a^{(1)}(i)\;\leq\;\frac{1}{t}\quad\forall t\geq T(\varepsilon)\ , (393) which also implies that τ t U ​ ( i ) t − a ( 1 ) ​ ( i ) &lt; K t ∀ t ≥ T ⁡ ( ε ) . \displaystyle\frac{\tau_{t}^{\rm U}(i)}{t}-a^{(1)}(i)\;&lt;\;\frac{K}{t}\quad\forall t\geq T(\varepsilon)\ . (394) Next, let us assume that there exists some j ∈ [ K ] j\in[K] such that τ t U ​ ( j ) t &lt; a ( 1 ) ​ ( j ) − K t . \displaystyle\frac{\tau_{t}^{\rm U}(j)}{t}&lt;a^{(1)}(j)-\frac{K}{t}\ . (395) In this case, we have ∑ i ∈ [ K ] τ t U ​ ( i ) t \displaystyle\sum\limits_{i\in[K]}\frac{\tau_{t}^{\rm U}(i)}{t} = ∑ i ≠ j τ t U ​ ( i ) t + τ t U ​ ( j ) t \displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i\neq j}\frac{\tau_{t}^{\rm U}(i)}{t}+\frac{\tau_{t}^{\rm U}(j)}{t} (396) ≤ ( 394 ) ∑ i ≠ j a ( 1 ) ​ ( i ) + K − 1 t + a ( 1 ) ​ ( j ) − K t \displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:UK_oneside}}}{{\leq}}\sum\limits_{i\neq j}a^{(1)}(i)+\frac{K-1}{t}+a^{(1)}(j)-\frac{K}{t} (397) = 1 − 1 t , \displaystyle=1-\frac{1}{t}\ , (398) which is a contradiction, since we should have ∑ i ∈ [ K ] τ t U ​ ( i ) t = 1 . \displaystyle\sum\limits_{i\in[K]}\frac{\tau_{t}^{\rm U}(i)}{t}\;=\;1\ . (399) Hence, we can conclude that | τ t U ​ ( i ) t − a ( 1 ) ​ ( i ) | &lt; K t ∀ t ≥ T ⁡ ( ε ) . \displaystyle\left|\frac{\tau_{t}^{\rm U}(i)}{t}-a^{(1)}(i)\right|&lt;\frac{K}{t}\quad\forall t\geq T(\varepsilon)\ . (400) Lemma 22 Under the event ℰ ¯ 3 , T \bar{\mathcal{E}}_{3,T} , there exists a finite time T ⁡ ( ε ) &lt; + ∞ T(\varepsilon)&lt;+\infty such that for every t &gt; T ⁡ ( ε ) t&gt;T(\varepsilon) , we have 𝒪 t = ∅ \mathcal{O}_{t}=\emptyset . Proof: For any time t &gt; T 0 ​ ( ε ) t&gt;T_{0}(\varepsilon) , and some arm j ∉ 𝒪 t j\notin\mathcal{O}_{t} , we have τ t U ​ ( j ) t ≤ a ( 1 ) ( j ) + 1 t . \displaystyle\frac{\tau_{t}^{\rm U}(j)}{t}\;\leq\;a^{(}1)(j)+\frac{1}{t}\ . (401) Based on this, we have the following two regimes of the arm selection fraction for arm j j . Case (a): ( τ t U ​ ( j ) / t ≤ α ¯ j ⋆ \tau_{t}^{\rm U}(j)/t\leq\bar{\alpha}_{j}^{\star} ). Since the PM-UCB-M algorithm only samples under-sampled arms, it is probable that the arm j j is sampled at time t + 1 t+1 . In that case, we would have τ t + 1 UCB ​ ( j ) t + 1 \displaystyle\frac{\tau_{t+1}^{\rm UCB}(j)}{t+1}\; ≤ τ t U ​ ( j ) + 1 t + 1 \displaystyle\leq\;\frac{\tau_{t}^{\rm U}(j)+1}{t+1} (402) = τ t U ​ ( j ) t + 1 + 1 t + 1 \displaystyle=\;\frac{\tau_{t}^{\rm U}(j)}{t+1}+\frac{1}{t+1} (403) ≤ a ( 1 ) ​ ( j ) ​ t t + 1 + 1 t + 1 \displaystyle\leq\;\frac{a^{(1)}(j)t}{t+1}+\frac{1}{t+1} (404) ≤ a ( 1 ) ​ ( j ) + 1 t + 1 , \displaystyle\leq\;a^{(1)}(j)+\frac{1}{t+1}\ , (405) where ( 404 ) follows from the fact that τ t U ​ ( j ) ≤ t ​ α ¯ j ⋆ \tau_{t}^{\rm U}(j)\leq t\bar{\alpha}_{j}^{\star} . Clearly, we see that the arm j j does not enter the set of over-sampled arms 𝒪 t \mathcal{O}_{t} in the subsequent round t + 1 t+1 . Case (b): ( α ¯ j ⋆ ≤ τ t U ​ ( j ) / t ≤ α ¯ j ⋆ + 1 / t \bar{\alpha}_{j}^{\star}\leq\tau_{t}^{\rm U}(j)/t\leq\bar{\alpha}_{j}^{\star}+1/t ). In this case, there always exists at least one arm k ∈ [ K ] k\in[K] which satisfies that τ t U ​ ( k ) / t ≤ a k ( 1 ) \tau_{t}^{\rm U}(k)/t\leq a^{(1)}_{k} . This implies that the arm j j is not sampled by the PM-UCB-M arm selection rule, since it is not the most under-sampled arm. In this case, we have τ t + 1 UCB ​ ( j ) t + 1 \displaystyle\frac{\tau_{t+1}^{\rm UCB}(j)}{t+1}\; = τ t U ​ ( j ) t + 1 \displaystyle=\;\frac{\tau_{t}^{\rm U}(j)}{t+1} (406) = τ t U ​ ( j ) t ​ t t + 1 \displaystyle=\;\frac{\tau_{t}^{\rm U}(j)}{t}\frac{t}{t+1} (407) ≤ ( a ( 1 ) ​ ( j ) + 1 t ) ​ t t + 1 \displaystyle\leq\;\left(a^{(1)}(j)+\frac{1}{t}\right)\frac{t}{t+1} (408) = a ( 1 ) ​ ( j ) ​ t t + 1 + 1 t + 1 \displaystyle=\;a^{(1)}(j)\frac{t}{t+1}+\frac{1}{t+1} (409) ≤ a ( 1 ) ​ ( j ) + 1 t + 1 , \displaystyle\;\leq a^{(1)}(j)+\frac{1}{t+1}\ , (410) where ( 408 ) from the definition of case (b). Again, we have concluded that j ∉ 𝒪 t + 1 j\notin\mathcal{O}_{t+1} . Combining ( 405 ) and ( 410 ), we conclude that none of the arms which are not already contained in the set 𝒪 t \mathcal{O}_{t} ever enters this set. Hence, all we are left to show is the existence of the time instant after which the set 𝒪 t \mathcal{O}_{t} becomes an empty set. Evidently, since the PM-UCB-M algorithm never samples from the set of over-sampled arms 𝒪 t \mathcal{O}_{t} , we will establish an upper bound m m such that after t &gt; T 0 ​ ( ε ) + m t&gt;T_{0}(\varepsilon)+m , all arms leave the set 𝒪 t \mathcal{O}_{t} . Notably, for any arm i ∈ 𝒪 t i\in\mathcal{O}_{t} , it holds that τ t U ​ ( i ) t &gt; a ( 1 ) ​ ( i ) + 1 t . \displaystyle\frac{\tau_{t}^{\rm U}(i)}{t}\;&gt;\;a^{(1)}(i)+\frac{1}{t}\ . (411) Let m m be such that τ T 0 ​ ( ε ) + m U ​ ( i ) T 0 ​ ( ε ) + m ≤ a ( 1 ) ​ ( i ) + 1 T 0 ​ ( ε ) + m , \displaystyle\frac{\tau_{T_{0}(\varepsilon)+m}^{\rm U}(i)}{T_{0}(\varepsilon)+m}\;\leq\;a^{(1)}(i)+\frac{1}{T_{0}(\varepsilon)+m}\ , (412) which implies that the arm i i has left the set 𝒪 T ⁡ ( ε ) + m \mathcal{O}_{T(\varepsilon)+m} . Furthermore, since arm i i is never sampled between times instants T ⁡ ( ε ) T(\varepsilon) and T ⁡ ( ε ) + m T(\varepsilon)+m (as it belongs to the over-sampled set), ( 412 ) can be equivalently written as τ T 0 ​ ( ε ) U ​ ( i ) T 0 ​ ( ε ) + m ≤ a ( 1 ) ​ ( i ) + 1 T 0 ​ ( ε ) + m . \displaystyle\frac{\tau_{T_{0}(\varepsilon)}^{\rm U}(i)}{T_{0}(\varepsilon)+m}\;\leq\;a^{(1)}(i)+\frac{1}{T_{0}(\varepsilon)+m}\ . Leveraging δ 12 ​ ( ε ) = Ω ⁡ ( ε β ) \delta_{12}(\varepsilon)=\Omega(\varepsilon^{\beta})\ , it can be readily verified that this choice of the discretization level ε \varepsilon satisfies the condition in ( 379 ). Hence, for arm selection regret, from ( K.1 ) we have ℜ ¯ 𝝂 , 1 C ​ ( T ) \displaystyle\bar{\mathfrak{R}}_{\boldsymbol{\nu},1}^{\rm C}(T) = O ⁡ ( ( K 2 q ​ log ⁡ T / T ) ( 1 − κ ) ​ q 2 ) . \displaystyle=O\left((K^{\frac{2}{q}}\log T/T)^{\left(1-\kappa\right)\frac{q}{2}}\right)\ . (452) Furthermore, from Lemma 8 , we have δ 01 ​ ( ε ) \displaystyle\delta_{01}(\varepsilon) ≤ ℒ ​ ( K ​ W ​ ε ) q . \displaystyle\leq\mathcal{L}(KW\varepsilon)^{q}\ . (453) Hence, δ 01 ​ ( ε ) = O ⁡ ( ε q ) \delta_{01}(\varepsilon)=O(\varepsilon^{q}) .
