(454) From ( 452 ) and ( 454 ) the regret of the CE-UCB-M is bounded from above by ℜ 𝝂 C ​ ( T ) \displaystyle\mathfrak{R}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}^{\rm C}(T) = O ⁡ ( max ⁡ { K q ⁡ ( 1 + 2 ​ κ q ) ​ ( log ⁡ T / T ) q ​ κ , ( K 2 q ​ log ⁡ T / T ) ( 1 − κ ) ​ q 2 } ) \displaystyle=O\left(\max\Big\{K^{q\left(1+\frac{2\kappa}{q}\right)}\left(\log T/T\right)^{q\kappa}\;,\;(K^{\frac{2}{q}}\log T/T)^{\left(1-\kappa\right)\frac{q}{2}}\Big\}\right) (455) = O ⁡ ( max ⁡ { K q ⁡ ( 1 + 2 ​ κ q ) , K 1 − κ } ​ max ​ { ( log ⁡ T / T ) q ​ κ , ( log ⁡ T / T ) ( 1 − κ ) ​ q 2 } ) . \displaystyle=O\left(\max\Big\{K^{q\left(1+\frac{2\kappa}{q}\right)},K^{1-\kappa}\Big\}\max\Big\{\left(\log T/T\right)^{q\kappa}\;,\;(\log T/T)^{(1-\kappa)\frac{q}{2}}\Big\}\right)\ \ . (456) As β ≥ q 2 \beta\geq\frac{q}{2} this bound becomes ℜ 𝝂 C ​ ( T ) ≤ O ⁡ ( max ⁡ { K q ⁡ ( 1 + 2 ​ κ q ) , K 1 − κ } ​ ( log ⁡ T / T ) q ​ κ ) . \displaystyle\mathfrak{R}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}^{\rm C}(T)\leq O\left(\max\Big\{K^{q\left(1+\frac{2\kappa}{q}\right)},K^{1-\kappa}\Big\}(\log T/T)^{q\kappa}\right)\ . (457) Appendix L CIRT Algorithm In this section, we provide the proofs for the performance guarantees corresponding to CIRT. This includes the proof of Lemma 5 (which provides an upper bound on the probability of error), followed by the proof of Lemma 7 (which shows the finiteness of the phase lengths), and finally, the proof of Theorem 9 , which upper bounds the regret due to the CIRT algorithm. L.1 Proof of Lemma 5 The proof of Lemma 5 contains two steps. The first step shows that an oracle strategy, which knows the arm distributions and performs adaptive discretization based on the true CDFs, will contain the optimal mixing coefficient 𝜶 ⋆ \boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\star} . The next step shows that the CIRT algorithm mimics the oracle strategy, which proves the claim of Lemma 5 . The first step holds for a K K -arm Bernoulli bandit instance. To show the first step, we begin by specifying the oracle refinement strategy. Definition 4 (Refinement Oracle) A refinement oracle ( RO {\rm RO} ) is defined as an algorithm that knows the arms’ CDFs { 𝔽 i : i ∈ [ K ] } \{\mathbb{F}_{i}:i\in[K]\} . Starting from Δ RO ( 0 ) = Δ K − 1 \Delta_{{\rm RO}}^{(0)}=\Delta^{K-1} , in each phase ℓ ∈ [ L ] \ell\in[L] , RO {\rm RO} chooses the best discrete mixing coefficient, which we denote by 𝐚 RO ( ℓ ) ∈ arg ​ max 𝐚 ∈ Δ RO ( ℓ ) ⁡ U ​ ( ∑ i ∈ [ K ] α ⁡ ( i ) ​ 𝔽 i ) , \displaystyle\mathbf{a}_{\rm RO}^{(\ell)}\;\in\;\argmax\limits_{\mathbf{a}\in\Delta^{(\ell)}_{\rm RO}}U\left(\sum\limits_{i\in[K]}\alpha(i)\mathbb{F}_{i}\right)\ , (458) and performs the interval refinement for the next phase ℓ + 1 \ell+1 based on 𝐚 RO ( ℓ ) \mathbf{a}_{\rm RO}^{(\ell)} , i.e., Δ RO ( ℓ + 1 ) ≜ { \displaystyle\Delta^{(\ell+1)}_{\rm RO}\triangleq\bigg\{ 𝐚 : n ∈ { ℕ ∪ 0 } , a ( i ) ≜ n 2 ℓ A ℓ + 1 , a ( i ) ∈ [ a RO ( ℓ ) ( i ) − 2 ℓ − 1 A ℓ , a RO ( ℓ ) ( i ) + 2 ℓ − 1 A ℓ ] , 1 T 𝐚 = 1 } . \displaystyle\mathbf{a}:\;\;n\in\{{\mathbb{N}}\cup{0}\},\;a(i)\triangleq n\frac{2^{\ell}}{A^{\ell+1}},\;a(i)\in\left[a_{\rm RO}^{(\ell)}(i)-\frac{2^{\ell-1}}{A^{\ell}},a_{\rm RO}^{(\ell)}(i)+\frac{2^{\ell-1}}{A^{\ell}}\right]\;,\;\mathbf{1}^{T}\mathbf{a}=1\bigg\}\ . The following result shows that for a K K -armed Bernoulli bandit, at the end of phase L L , the optimal mixing coefficient is contained in the refined interval due to RO. Lemma 23 Consider a K K -armed Bernoulli bandit instance 𝛎 ≜ { Bern ⁡ ( p ⁡ ( 1 ) ) , ⋯ , Bern ⁡ ( p ⁡ ( K ) ) } \boldsymbol{\nu}\triangleq\{{\rm Bern}(p(1)),\cdots,{\rm Bern}(p(K))\} , and let 𝐩 ≜ [ p ⁡ ( 1 ) , ⋯ , p ⁡ ( K ) ] ⊤ \mathbf{p}\triangleq[p(1),\cdots,p(K)]^{\top} denote the vector of mean values. For a PM with a solitary arm as the optimal solution, when RO performs interval refinement for L L phases on 𝝂 \boldsymbol{\nu} , we have 𝜶 𝝂 ⋆ ∈ Σ RO ( L ) \boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\star}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\in\Sigma^{(L)}_{\rm{RO}} . Proof: We will prove the claim by induction on the phase ℓ \ell . Let i ⋆ i^{\star} denote the optimal arm, i.e., i ⋆ ∈ arg ​ max i ∈ [ K ] ⁡ U h ​ ( 𝔽 i ) . \displaystyle i^{\star}\;\in\;\argmax_{i\in[K]}\;U_{h}(\mathbb{F}_{i})\ . (459) Base case ( ℓ = 0 \ell=0 ): As the base case, it can be readily verified that 𝐚 RO ( 1 ) ≜ 𝐞 i ⋆ \mathbf{a}^{(1)}_{\rm{RO}}\triangleq\mathbf{e}_{i^{\star}} , where we let 𝐞 i \mathbf{e}_{i} denote the unit vector with an i th i^{\rm th} non-zero coordinate. Hence, we conclude that 𝐚 RO ( 1 ) ∈ Δ RO ( 1 ) \mathbf{a}^{(1)}_{\rm{RO}}\in\Delta_{\rm{RO}}^{(1)} . Inductive hypothesis: For any ℓ &lt; L \ell&lt;L , let us assume that 𝐞 i ⋆ ∈ Δ RO ( ℓ ) \mathbf{e}_{i^{\star}}\in\Delta_{\rm RO}^{(\ell)} . Induction step: Owing to the interval refinement step defined in ( 4 ), it can be readily verified that 𝐞 i ⋆ ∈ Δ RO ( ℓ + 1 ) \mathbf{e}_{i^{\star}}\in\Delta_{\rm{RO}}^{(\ell+1)} . Hence, we can conclude that 𝐞 i ⋆ ∈ Δ RO ( L ) \mathbf{e}_{i^{\star}}\in\Delta_{\rm RO}^{(L)} . Lemma 24 Consider a K K -armed Bernoulli bandit instance with mean vector 𝐩 ≜ [ p ⁡ ( 1 ) , ⋯ , p ⁡ ( K ) ] ⊤ \mathbf{p}\triangleq[p(1),\cdots,p(K)]^{\top} . For a PM with concave distortion function which has a mixture for its optimal solution, when RO performs interval refinement for L L phases on 𝝂 \boldsymbol{\nu} , we have 𝜶 𝝂 ⋆ ∈ Δ RO ( L ) \boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\star}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\in\Delta^{(L)}_{\rm{RO}} . We will prove that, given the optimal mixing coefficient is contained in the interval refined by the RO in the current phase ℓ \ell (denoted by Δ RO ( ℓ ) \Delta_{\rm{RO}}^{(\ell)} ), it would also be contained in the refined interval of the next phase, i.e., Δ RO ( ℓ + 1 ) \Delta_{\rm{RO}}^{(\ell+1)} . Subsequently, following the same line of arguments as Lemma 23 , using induction, the statement of the lemma readily follows. Let us begin by assuming that 𝜶 𝝂 ⋆ ∈ Δ RO ( ℓ ) \boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\star}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\in\Delta^{(\ell)}_{\rm{RO}} . For a K K -arm Bernoulli bandit instance 𝝂 \boldsymbol{\nu} with mean values 𝐩 = [ p ⁡ ( 1 ) , … , p ⁡ ( K ) ] \mathbf{p}=[p(1),\dots,p(K)] , and for any mixing coefficient 𝐚 ∈ Δ K − 1 \mathbf{a}\in\Delta^{K-1} we have the following relationship: U h ​ ( ∑ i ∈ [ K ] a ⁡ ( i ) ​ 𝔽 i ) = h ⁡ ( ∑ i ∈ [ K ] a ⁡ ( i ) ​ p ​ ( i ) ) . \displaystyle U_{h}\left(\sum_{i\in[K]}a(i)\mathbb{F}_{i}\right)=h\left(\sum_{i\in[K]}a(i)p(i)\right)\ . (460) Without loss of generality, let us assume that ∑ i ∈ [ K ] α ⋆ ​ ( i ) ​ p ​ ( i ) ≤ ∑ i ∈ [ K ] a RO ( ℓ ) ​ ( i ) ​ p ​ ( i ) . \displaystyle\sum_{i\in[K]}\alpha^{\star}(i)p(i)\leq\sum_{i\in[K]}a^{(\ell)}_{\rm{RO}}(i)p(i)\ . (461) Now, we will show that for any 𝐚 ∈ Δ RO ( ℓ ) \mathbf{a}\in\Delta_{\rm{RO}}^{(\ell)} such that ∑ i ∈ [ K ] a ⁡ ( i ) ​ p ​ ( i ) &lt; ∑ i ∈ [ K ] a RO ( ℓ ) ​ ( i ) ​ p ​ ( i ) , \displaystyle\sum_{i\in[K]}a(i)p(i)&lt;\sum_{i\in[K]}a^{(\ell)}_{\rm{RO}}(i)p(i)\ , (462) due to the concavity of the distortion function, we have ∑ i ∈ [ K ] a ⁡ ( i ) ​ p ​ ( i ) &lt; ∑ i ∈ [ K ] α ⋆ ​ ( i ) ​ p ​ ( i ) . \displaystyle\sum_{i\in[K]}a(i)p(i)&lt;\sum_{i\in[K]}\alpha^{\star}(i)p(i)\ . (463) We will prove this claim by contradiction. Let us assume that ∑ i ∈ [ K ] a ⁡ ( i ) ​ p ​ ( i ) ≥ ∑ i ∈ [ K ] α ⋆ ​ ( i ) ​ p ​ ( i ) \sum_{i\in[K]}a(i)p(i)\geq\sum_{i\in[K]}\alpha^{\star}(i)p(i) . For any λ ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \lambda\in(0,1) we have h ⁡ ( ∑ i ∈ [ K ] a ⁡ ( i ) ​ p ​ ( i ) ) \displaystyle h\left(\sum_{i\in[K]}a(i)p(i)\right) ≥ λ ​ h ​ ( ∑ i ∈ [ K ] α ⋆ ​ ( i ) ​ p ​ ( i ) ) + ( 1 − λ ) ​ h ​ ( ∑ i ∈ [ K ] a RO ( ℓ ) ​ ( i ) ​ p ​ ( i ) ) \displaystyle\geq\lambda h\left(\sum_{i\in[K]}\alpha^{\star}(i)p(i)\right)+(1-\lambda)h\left(\sum_{i\in[K]}a^{(\ell)}_{\rm{RO}}(i)p(i)\right) (464) &gt; min ⁡ { h ⁡ ( ∑ i ∈ [ K ] α ⋆ ​ ( i ) ​ p ​ ( i ) ) , h ⁡ ( ∑ i ∈ [ K ] a RO ( ℓ ) ​ ( i ) ​ p ​ ( i ) ) } \displaystyle&gt;\min\left\{h\left(\sum_{i\in[K]}\alpha^{\star}(i)p(i)\right)\;,\;h\left(\sum_{i\in[K]}a^{(\ell)}_{\rm{RO}}(i)p(i)\right)\right\} (465) = h ⁡ ( ∑ i ∈ [ K ] a RO ( ℓ ) ​ ( i ) ​ p ​ ( i ) ) , \displaystyle=h\left(\sum_{i\in[K]}a^{(\ell)}_{\rm{RO}}(i)p(i)\right)\ , (466) where ( 464 ) follows from the concavity of h h , and, ( 466 ) follows from the definition of 𝜶 ⋆ \boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\star} . It can be readily verified that ( 466 ) contradicts the definition of 𝐚 RO ( ℓ ) \mathbf{a}^{(\ell)}_{\rm{RO}} . Therefore, we can conclude that ∑ i ∈ [ K ] a ⁡ ( i ) ​ p ​ ( i ) ≤ ∑ i ∈ [ K ] α ⋆ ​ ( i ) ​ p ​ ( i ) \sum_{i\in[K]}a(i)p(i)\leq\sum_{i\in[K]}\alpha^{\star}(i)p(i) . Consequently, we we can express 𝜶 ⋆ \boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\star} as a convex combination of any such discrete mixing coefficient 𝐚 \mathbf{a} , and the best discrete mixing coefficient 𝐚 RO ( ℓ ) \mathbf{a}_{\rm{RO}}^{(\ell)} for λ ∈ [ 0 , 1 ) \lambda\in[0,1) , i.e., 𝜶 ⋆ = λ ​ 𝐚 RO ( ℓ ) + ( 1 − λ ) ​ 𝐚 . \displaystyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\star}=\lambda\mathbf{a}^{(\ell)}_{\rm{RO}}+(1-\lambda)\mathbf{a}\ . (467) We choose 𝐚 = { a RO ( ℓ ) ​ ( i ) − 1 / A ℓ , i = m , a RO ( ℓ ) ​ ( i ) + 1 / A ℓ , i = n , p ( m ) &gt; p ( n ) , a RO ( ℓ ) ​ ( i ) , i ≠ m , n . \displaystyle\mathbf{a}=\begin{cases}a^{(\ell)}_{\rm{RO}}(i)-1/A^{\ell},&amp;i=m\ ,\\ a^{(\ell)}_{\rm{RO}}(i)+1/A^{\ell},&amp;i=n,p(m)&gt;p(n)\ ,\\ a^{(\ell)}_{\rm{RO}}(i),&amp;i\neq{m,n}\ .\end{cases} (468) This choice ensures that ∑ i ∈ [ K ] a ⁡ ( i ) ​ p ​ ( i ) &lt; ∑ i ∈ [ K ] a RO ( ℓ ) ​ ( i ) ​ p ​ ( i ) . \displaystyle\sum_{i\in[K]}a(i)p(i)&lt;\sum_{i\in[K]}a^{(\ell)}_{\rm{RO}}(i)p(i)\ . (469) Hence, we have 𝜶 ⋆ = { a RO ( ℓ ) ​ ( i ) − ( 1 − λ ) ​ 1 / A ℓ , i = m , a RO ( ℓ ) ​ ( i ) + ( 1 − λ ) ​ 1 / A ℓ , i = n , p ( m ) &gt; p ( n ) , a RO ( ℓ ) ​ ( i ) , i ≠ m , n . \displaystyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\star}=\begin{cases}a^{(\ell)}_{\rm{RO}}(i)-(1-\lambda)1/A^{\ell},&amp;i=m\ ,\\ a^{(\ell)}_{\rm{RO}}(i)+(1-\lambda)1/A^{\ell},&amp;i=n,p(m)&gt;p(n)\ ,\\ a^{(\ell)}_{\rm{RO}}(i),&amp;i\neq{m,n}\ .\end{cases} (470) This proves that 𝜶 ⋆ ∈ Δ RO ( ℓ + 1 ) \boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\star}\in\Delta^{(\ell+1)}_{\rm{RO}} , based on the interval refinement stated in ( 4 ). This completes our proof. Lemma 25 For a K K -arm Bernoulli bandit instance with mean values 𝐩 = [ p ⁡ ( 1 ) , ⋯ , p ⁡ ( K ) ] \mathbf{p}=[p(1),\cdots,p(K)] , when T &gt; τ ( L ) T&gt;\tau^{(L)} the CIRT algorithm results in an optimal mixing coefficient being contained in Σ ϵ K − 1 \Sigma^{K-1}_{\epsilon} with a high probability, i.e., ℙ 𝝂 IR ​ ( 𝜶 ⋆ ∉ Σ ϵ K − 1 ) ≤ L ​ δ . \displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}^{\rm IR}\Big(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\star}\notin\Sigma_{\epsilon}^{K-1}\Big)\;\leq\;L\delta\ . (471) Proof: From the Lemmas 23 and 24 , we have obtained that ℙ 𝝂 IR ​ ( 𝜶 ⋆ ∈ Σ ϵ K − 1 ) ≥ ℙ 𝝂 IR ​ ( ⋂ ℓ = 1 L 𝐚 ( ℓ ) = 𝐚 RO ( ℓ ) ) . \displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}^{\rm IR}\left(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\star}\in\Sigma_{\epsilon}^{K-1}\right)\geq{\mathbb{P}}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}^{\rm IR}\left(\bigcap_{\ell=1}^{L}\mathbf{a}^{(\ell)}=\mathbf{a}^{(\ell)}_{\rm{RO}}\right)\ . (472) Here, the inequality results from the fact that choosing a RO ( ℓ ) a^{(\ell)}_{\rm{RO}} to zoom into the simplex at each stage ℓ ∈ [ L ] \ell\in[L] results in one of the optimal solutions 𝜶 ⋆ \boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\star} to be contained in the last simplex Δ RO ( L ) \Delta_{\rm{RO}}^{(L)} , and there could be possibly more optimal mixing coefficients. Let us define the event S ≜ ⋂ ℓ = 1 L { 𝐚 ( ℓ ) = 𝐚 RO ( ℓ ) } S\triangleq\bigcap_{\ell=1}^{L}\{\mathbf{a}^{(\ell)}=\mathbf{a}^{(\ell)}_{\rm{RO}}\} . It can be readily verified that S ¯ = ⋃ ℓ ∈ [ L ] { { 𝐚 ( ℓ ) ≠ 𝐚 RO ( ℓ ) } ⋂ ℓ ′ ∈ [ ℓ ] { 𝐚 ( ℓ ′ ) = 𝐚 RO ( ℓ ′ ) } } . \displaystyle\bar{S}\;=\;\bigcup_{\ell\in[L]}\left\{\Big\{\mathbf{a}^{(\ell)}\neq\mathbf{a}^{(\ell)}_{\rm{RO}}\Big\}\bigcap_{\ell^{\prime}\in[\ell]}\Big\{\mathbf{a}^{(\ell^{\prime})}=\mathbf{a}^{(\ell^{\prime})}_{\rm{RO}}\Big\}\right\}\ . (473) Next, we will provide an upper bound on the probability of error, i.e., ℙ 𝝂 IR ​ ( 𝜶 ⋆ ∉ Σ ϵ K − 1 ) {\mathbb{P}}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}^{\rm IR}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\star}\notin\Sigma_{\epsilon}^{K-1}) . Note that leveraging ( 472 ) and ( 473 ), it can be readily verified that ℙ 𝝂 IR ​ ( 𝜶 ⋆ ∉ Σ L K − 1 ) \displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}^{\rm IR}\left(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\star}\notin\Sigma_{L}^{K-1}\right) ≤ ℙ 𝝂 IR ( ⋃ ℓ ∈ [ L ] { 𝐚 ( ℓ ) ≠ 𝐚 RO ( ℓ ′ ) } ⋂ ℓ ′ ∈ [ ℓ ] { 𝐚 ( ℓ ′ ) = 𝐚 RO ( ℓ ′ ) } } ) \displaystyle\leq{\mathbb{P}}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}^{\rm IR}\left(\bigcup_{\ell\in[L]}\left\{\mathbf{a}^{(\ell)}\neq\mathbf{a}^{(\ell^{\prime})}_{\rm{RO}}\}\bigcap_{\ell^{\prime}\in[\ell]}\{\mathbf{a}^{(\ell^{\prime})}=\mathbf{a}^{(\ell^{\prime})}_{\rm{RO}}\}\right\}\right) (474) ≤ ∑ ℓ ∈ [ L ] ℙ 𝝂 IR ( 𝐚 ( ℓ ) ≠ 𝐚 RO ( ℓ ) ⋂ ℓ ′ ∈ [ ℓ ] { 𝐚 ( ℓ ′ ) = 𝐚 RO ( ℓ ′ ) } ) . \displaystyle\leq\sum_{\ell\in[L]}{\mathbb{P}}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}^{\rm IR}\left(\mathbf{a}^{(\ell)}\neq\mathbf{a}^{(\ell)}_{\rm{RO}}\bigcap_{\ell^{\prime}\in[\ell]}\{\mathbf{a}^{(\ell^{\prime})}=\mathbf{a}^{(\ell^{\prime})}_{\rm{RO}}\}\right)\ . (475) For any time t ∈ ℕ t\in{\mathbb{N}} , let us define the event ℰ 0 , t ≜ { ∀ i ∈ [ K ] , 𝔽 i ∈ C t ( i ) } . \displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{0,t}\triangleq\Big\{\forall i\in[K],\mathbb{F}_{i}\in C_{t}(i)\Big\}\ . (476) By the design of the CIRT confidence sets in ( 44 ), it can be readily verified that ℙ 𝝂 IR ​ ( ℰ ¯ 0 , t ) ≤ δ . \displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}^{\rm IR}\left(\bar{\mathcal{E}}_{0,t}\right)\;\leq\;\delta\ . (477) We will now bound each probability term under the summand in ( 475 ). We begin with the first phase, i.e., ℓ = 1 \ell=1 . (526) L.3.1 Upper bound on the CIRT estimation regret Note that the number of phases L L is determined based on the allowable precision ϵ \epsilon , i.e., ( 2 / A ) L &lt; ϵ (2/A)^{L}&lt;\epsilon . Based on this, the estimation regret of the CIRT algorithm is upper-bounded as follows. (534) Next, we will show that for the sampling rule defined in ( 52 ), after a finite time instant, an arm is sampled only if its under-sampled . Note that for any arm i ∈ [ K ] i\in[K] , and at any time instant t ∈ ℕ t\in{\mathbb{N}} , we may have the following scenarios. • Case 1: arm i i may be under-explored and under-sampled, • Case 2: arm i i may be sufficiently explored but under-sampled, • Case 3: arm i i may be under-explored but not under-sampled, and, • Case 4: arm i i may be sufficiently explored and not under-sampled. We will show that after a finite time instant, the third case cannot happen. Let us assume that there is an arm that is not under-sampled but under-explored, which implies that i ∈ E t i\in E_{t} , or equivalently, τ t IR ​ ( i ) ≤ ⌈ ( t K ) 1 1 + ξ ⌉ = ( t K ) 1 1 + ξ + 1 . \displaystyle\tau_{t}^{\rm IR}(i)\leq\left\lceil\left(\frac{t}{K}\right)^{\frac{1}{1+\xi}}\right\rceil\ =\left(\frac{t}{K}\right)^{\frac{1}{1+\xi}}+1\ . (535) Furthermore, we have a t IR ​ ( i ) &lt; τ t IR ​ ( i ) t . \displaystyle a^{\rm IR}_{t}(i)&lt;\frac{\tau_{t}^{\rm IR}(i)}{t}\ . Next, we will show that the CIRT arm selection routine, using the under-sampling procedure, eventually converges to an optimal mixture distribution.
